# DreamyCafe POS — Changelog

### Fixed
- **A reboot proved the whole LAN TLS stack comes back unattended, and reading the boot logs caught two comments in [Caddyfile](Caddyfile) that would have misled the next person — one of them the same false claim already corrected in the docs.** The reboot was the one home-side verification never performed, and it passed cleanly: from power-on at 23:19:56, **Postgres at +13 s, the app at +15 s, Caddy at +25 s**, so the `DependOnService DreamyCafeApp` ordering held with nobody logged in. Static IP still `192.168.0.10/24 Manual` on `Ethernet 2`; **both names returned 200 with the chain verified**; all three firewall rules intact including the 3000 block (Caddy owns 80/443, node owns 3000 but is blocked inbound); `verify-resilience.ps1` all-green; all five scheduled tasks `Ready` with last result `0`. 🔴 **The keystone was verified rather than inferred**, because the session table was ambiguous — the console session showed *no* username, and an active RDP session could have been the thing creating the interactive session. `explorer.exe` settles it: started **as `User` 14 seconds after boot**, long before any remote login was possible, with `G:\My Drive` visible in that session. That matters because all four `LogonType Interactive` tasks are invisible to session 0 — had autologon not fired, backups would have silently stopped. [lock-on-logon.log](logs/lock-on-logon.log) also shows the **uptime guard discriminating correctly** in both directions: `console session locked after autologon (booted 0.4 min ago)` on this boot, and `skipped - booted 9.1 min ago (> 5), so this is a human logon` on earlier ones — the behaviour a `SESSIONNAME` check would get backwards. **Fix 1:** the Caddyfile header still asserted "The mini-PC itself is trusted automatically when Caddy runs as the SYSTEM service" — the exact falsehood corrected in [lan-tls.md](docs/lan-tls.md), missed in this copy. It now records that the installer imports the root itself, that a healthy start logs `root certificate is already trusted by system` (which this boot did, confirming the import **persists** rather than needing a re-run), and that `curl -k` must never be used to smoke-test it because it passes on a broken chain. **Fix 2:** Caddy warns `Unnecessary header_up X-Forwarded-For` on **every start**, and it is wrong for this threat model — a warning practically engineered to get "cleaned up" later. Caddy's default **appends** the real IP *after* any client-sent value, and [clientIp()](src/lib/ratelimit.js#L28) reads `fwd.split(',')[0]` — the **first** entry — so without the explicit overwrite a tablet could send `X-Forwarded-For: 9.9.9.9`, be read as `9.9.9.9`, and rotate it to dodge the per-tablet PIN lockout. The line is now annotated as load-bearing with the consequence of removing it stated inline. Comments only, and `caddy validate` confirms `Valid configuration`. Remaining cosmetic noise left alone deliberately: `Caddyfile input is not formatted` is `caddy fmt` pedantry about a blank line, not a defect.

### Added
- **LAN TLS is installed, running and chain-verified, the static IP is set, and the certificate deliberately covers a hostname as well as the IP so the tablets survive the address changing.** With the iPads not at home, the expensive mistake to design out was **picking the wrong IP**: a cert issued only for an address is *pinned* to it, so a clash with the shop's DHCP pool would mean a certificate error on every tablet and a lap of the shop re-trusting each one by hand. The [Caddyfile](Caddyfile) now serves **two site addresses** — `{$POS_LAN_HOST}` and `{$POS_LAN_NAME:dreamy-cafe.local}` — and [install-caddy-service.ps1](scripts/install-caddy-service.ps1) gained a `-LanName` parameter defaulting to this box's own name plus `.local`, passed through to the service environment. Caddy issues a **separate cert per name** (`192.168.0.10.crt` with `IP Address=192.168.0.10`, `dreamy-cafe.local.crt` with `DNS Name=dreamy-cafe.local`) from the **same** `Caddy Local Authority - ECC Intermediate`, so one root trust on the iPad still covers both. Net effect: an address change becomes one command instead of a tablet-by-tablet re-trust, which is why the tablets should be bookmarked to the **name** and the IP kept as the fallback — the reverse of the usual advice, and correct here because the IP is the thing most likely to move. **Static IP set to `192.168.0.10/24`**, gateway and DNS `192.168.0.1`, on **`Ethernet 2`** (the adapter the shop will actually use, not the Wi-Fi dongle). `.10` chosen because low addresses sit below most consumer DHCP pools, `.12` and `.4` are the printers, and nothing observed on the LAN (`.1 .22 .88 .101 .195 .230`) uses it; the script **pings the target first** and aborts rather than claim an address something else holds. ⚠ Two things checked before touching the network, because both would have been ugly: the DHCP lease was only **2 hours** (22:03 → 00:03), confirming the old `.22` really would have drifted; and `qwinsta` showed an **active RDP session** — but it rides the **Tailscale** interface (`100.105.157.110`), not `Ethernet 2`, so changing the LAN address could not drop it. `DREAMY-CAFE.local` re-resolved to `.10` immediately afterwards, which is what makes the hostname approach viable at all. Caddy is registered as `DreamyCafeCaddy` (auto-start, depends on `DreamyCafeApp`), firewall now **allows 443/80 and BLOCKS 3000**, and both names return **200 on `/api/health` with the chain verified**. 🟠 Still unverified and deliberately not claimed: whether the shop's router passes **mDNS** — if it blocks Bonjour, `.local` will not resolve there and the tablets fall back to the IP. Also written: [docs/ipad-till-setup.md](docs/ipad-till-setup.md), the shop-side steps only, with a pre-flight address check, the Certificate-Trust-Settings toggle called out as the single most-missed step, the cookie-hardening ordering trap, and an "if the address has to change" section that ends in *the tablets do not need redoing*. The static IP also **unblocked a stale placeholder**: [staff-quick-reference.md](docs/staff-quick-reference.md) had been telling staff to open `http://192.168.1.10:3000` — wrong subnet, wrong scheme, wrong port — and now reads `https://dreamy-cafe.local`.

### Fixed
- **🔴 Caddy silently failed to trust its own root on this machine, and `docs/lan-tls.md` asserted the opposite — a claim that would have made the install look verified when it was not.** The guide stated the mini-PC is trusted automatically because "Caddy installs its root into the machine store when it runs as the SYSTEM service". It **tries and fails**, and records it only in its own stderr: `pki.ca.local "failed to install root certificate" error="add cert failed: Failed adding cert: The request is not supported."` — Caddy's trust-store install does not work when it runs as a Windows **service** under SYSTEM. **The failure mode is what makes this worth fixing rather than noting:** `curl -k` returns **200** and reads as success, while `curl` without `-k` and every .NET client return nothing and fail chain validation. The installer's own smoke test used `-k`, so it would have reported a healthy install over a broken trust chain indefinitely — and I only caught it because verifying "both names work" with `-k` proves nothing about whether the *cert covers* those names, so I re-ran it without. Two changes: [install-caddy-service.ps1](scripts/install-caddy-service.ps1) now **imports the root into `Cert:\LocalMachine\Root` itself**, idempotently (re-running reports `already trusted`), and its end-to-end check uses `Invoke-WebRequest` **without** `-k` against **both** names, so a broken chain fails the check instead of passing it. The vestigial curl branch was removed and the port test kept as a secondary signal, to distinguish "Caddy isn't listening" from "listening but the name or chain is wrong". Verified by re-running the whole installer: `Root CA already trusted`, both names `200 (chain verified)`, `TLS port 443 listening: True`. The false claim in [lan-tls.md](docs/lan-tls.md) is replaced with the actual error text and an explanation of why `-k` hides it. **Nothing about the iPad steps changes** — the tablets were always going to need the manual trust; it was only this box that was wrong about itself.

### Fixed
- **The domain turns out not to be on the critical path to opening at all, and tracing that produced two corrections — one of them a silent failure that re-prioritises Caddy from "nice to have" to "do this before opening".** The question was whether the domain is the last major blocker. It is not, and the useful reframe is that **"production" is two independent tracks**: the in-store POS, which takes money on day one and has **zero domain dependency**, and online ordering, which is entirely domain-gated. **The in-store claim was verified rather than assumed**, because it would have been easy to get wrong: in-store Square goes through [/api/terminal/status](src/app/api/terminal/status/route.js), which **polls** `provider.getStatus(paymentIntentId)`. It is not webhook-driven, so it needs no public URL, no tunnel and no domain — which is also why the owner's live test charge succeeded days before any of that exists. Webhooks are an **online-checkout** dependency only. 🔴 **Correction 1, and the important one: Caddy LAN TLS is load-bearing and fails silently.** It had been filed as Phase 9 polish. But the app registers a **service worker** ([ServiceWorkerRegister.js](src/components/shared/ServiceWorkerRegister.js)), and browsers expose `navigator.serviceWorker` **only in a secure context** — `https://` or `localhost`. A LAN IP over plain `http://` is neither, so on the iPad `'serviceWorker' in navigator` is **false**, the component's own guard returns early, and `.register('/sw.js').catch(() => {})` swallows anything that gets further. The result is a till with **no offline cache that never reaches `offline.html`**: a Wi-Fi blip mid-order shows the barista a browser error page instead of the app, and **nothing reports that the PWA layer is inert**. That also means the entire `sw.js` cache-versioning effort — including the `git safe.directory` fix that stopped the cache key regenerating on every service start — buys nothing until TLS exists. Caddy is now recorded as the critical-path item immediately after the static IP, which gates it because the certificate must match the address the iPad actually uses. 🟠 **Correction 2, to my own earlier report:** I had listed "no DreamyCafe firewall rule exists" as an open item, which was true but incomplete and left the wrong impression. There **are** two auto-created `Node.js JavaScript Runtime` inbound allow rules covering `node.exe`, and the app listens on `0.0.0.0:3000` — so a tablet can reach the till **today**. `scripts\allow-lan-pos.ps1` is therefore **hardening, not enablement**: the existing rules allow `node.exe` on **any port** across both the Private *and* **Public** profiles, which is broader than a POS needs. Downgraded from blocker to hardening.

### Changed
- **The mini-PC was profiled for longevity, and the measurement overturned the premise: SSD wear is a non-issue by three orders of magnitude, so most of what an "optimise Windows for SSD life" pass would have changed was deliberately left alone.** The codebase has had a lot of attention and the host OS none, so the box was measured before anything was touched. `Get-StorageReliabilityCounter` reports the Toshiba KXG6AZNV256G at **`Wear: 0`, 27 °C, HealthStatus Healthy**. Write attribution was done through `Win32_Process.WriteTransferCount ÷ process age` — CIM rather than `Get-Process`, for the same reason [verify-resilience.ps1](scripts/verify-resilience.ps1) uses CIM: it needs no process handle and so reads SYSTEM-owned processes unelevated. **The first result was misleading and is worth recording as a method note.** Whole-machine write rate came to **1,319 MB/h → 11 TB/year**, which looks alarming until you read the ranking: `Code.exe` 837 MB/h, `chrome.exe` 51, `claude.exe` 13 — the top writers are *this workstation session*, none of which exists on a headless POS. Re-measuring only the production processes (`node.exe`, `postgres.exe`, `GoogleDriveFS.exe`, `nssm.exe`) gives **3.11 MB/h → 0.073 GB/day → 0.026 TB/year**, or **~3,800 years** against a conservative 100 TBW rating. The POS's own Next.js and PostgreSQL processes did not appear in the top 18 writers at all. **So the folklore was measured and rejected item by item**, and the numbers are recorded so it does not get re-litigated: Windows Search **1.97 MB/h**, SysMain **2.65**, DiagTrack **1.6**, WpnService **2.59** — about **9 MB/h combined**, noise against a 1,300 MB/h machine total. Also deliberately **not** changed, each for a specific reason rather than caution: the **page file** (peak use `0.04 GB` of `2.88 GB` allocated — it is already writing nothing, and removing it breaks crash dumps), **System Restore** (capped at 4.73 GB / 1%, and it is cheap rollback insurance for the *unattended* 04:00 patch reboot, which makes it more valuable on this box, not less), **`ScheduledDefrag`** (on an SSD this issues **TRIM/retrim** — the guides that tell you to disable it are wrong for SSDs, and `DisableDeleteNotify = 0` confirms TRIM is already reaching the drive), and **`DisableLastAccess`** (Windows already throttles it to once per hour per file, so the classic win is mostly gone). 🔴 **Two PostgreSQL settings are called out as permanently off-limits:** `synchronous_commit` and `full_page_writes` both stay `on`. Turning either off benchmarks dramatically faster and trades away the last committed transactions — or a torn page — on power loss. This box has **no UPS by design** and takes card payments, so a charged customer whose order vanished is not a performance trade available to us. Both were re-read and confirmed `on` after the one change that *was* made. **Two of my own suspicions were killed by evidence rather than confirmed**, which is the reason to measure first: the Security event log looked like it was churning at 26 MB/h, but it holds **827 hours — 34 days — of history in 20 MB**, so that rate was transient (today's reboot, Windows Update, and the auditing itself); and the `svchost` writing 133 MB/h turned out not to be telemetry or indexing but **`DoSvc`**, which is a genuine finding in its own right (below).

### Fixed
- **Delivery Optimization was the single largest disk writer on the machine and was set, by Windows default, to upload update content to strangers over the shop's internet connection — the same link that carries Square card authorisations.** Neither `HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\DeliveryOptimization` nor its Policies key existed, and absent a policy the default download mode is **peer-to-peer (HTTP + LAN + internet peering)**. Measured at **132.7 MB/h**, `DoSvc` out-wrote every other process on the box — including the POS itself — by a factor of ~40. **The reason to change it is bandwidth, not wear:** 133 MB/h is still only ~1.1 TB/year against a 100 TBW drive, so this is not a longevity fix; it is that an upload burst during service competes with a card authorisation, and a payment that spins is the expensive failure. Now set in [configure-windows-updates.ps1](scripts/configure-windows-updates.ps1) as a new section 5: **`DODownloadMode = 0`** (Delivery Optimization's download manager is kept for its resume and throttling behaviour, but peering is off entirely — HTTP from Microsoft only, nothing shared outward) and **`DOPercentageMaxBackgroundBandwidth = 40`**, exposed as `-MaxUpdateBandwidthPercent` with an `-AllowUpdatePeering` escape hatch for a shop that deliberately wants the default. The cap applies to **background** traffic only, so a manually-triggered update still runs at full speed when someone is deliberately waiting for it. **Applied and verified:** `DoSvc` restarted and now reports **1.25 MB/h**, and free space went from **198.3 GB to 200.8 GB**. ⚠ **Honest attribution: the 133 → 1.25 drop is not all policy.** The original figure was sampled while today's Windows Update work was actually running, so part of the fall is simply that nothing is downloading now. The policy is correct on its own terms — no outward sharing, capped background use — and should not be credited with the whole delta.
- **The monthly maintenance job now tracks SSD *temperature*, which on this hardware is the metric that matters, and prunes the Windows temp folders, which nothing else does.** [db-maintenance.ps1](scripts/db-maintenance.ps1) already read `Wear`, `PowerOnHours` and uncorrected error counts, but **not `Temperature`** — and the write profiling above inverts which of those deserves attention. Wear is a ~3,800-year problem; heat is a real one, because this is a Mini/DM chassis with a single small blower fan destined for a café, where grease and flour clog an intake in a way an office never does, and heat is what kills NVMe controllers and nearby capacitors. Temperature now appears in every log line (`temp=27C` on the first run) with a two-tier response matching the existing wear tiers: **60 °C logs a "check the fan and vents" warning**, **70 °C raises the same alarm as a failing drive** (these drives throttle around 70–80 °C). The threshold matters less than the direction, which is why the reading goes to `logs\db-maintenance.log` every run — a slow climb over a year is the actual signal, and it is invisible unless each reading is kept. **Second addition: temp-folder pruning.** Storage Sense is off by default on Pro and nothing else clears these, so by **day two** of this build there was **933 MB in `C:\Windows\Temp` and 911 MB in the user's Temp** — 1.8 GB of creep. Disk creep is the mechanism that eventually trips `backup-db.ps1`'s 5 GB free-space alarm, which is the one failure that stops PostgreSQL writing and therefore **stops sales**. The prune only touches files older than 7 days so a running installer's scratch files are never pulled out from under it, and every delete is swallowed per-file because a locked temp file is normal and must not fail the run. **First real run freed 775.9 MB.** ⚠ **One behaviour was found during that run and made explicit rather than left as a trap:** `C:\Windows\Temp` reported *"removed 0 file(s), 0 in use"* against a folder holding 933 MB — because most of it is SYSTEM-owned and an unelevated shell cannot even **enumerate** it, so `-ErrorAction SilentlyContinue` swallowed the failure at the listing stage and the pass looked clean rather than blocked. The scheduled task runs `RunLevel=Highest` and does clear it; a manual run from a normal window will not. The script now detects elevation and logs exactly that, so nobody reads a silent zero as a broken prune.
- **PostgreSQL's `random_page_cost` was still `4` — a spinning-disk assumption on an NVMe box.** The default encodes "a random read costs 4× a sequential one because the head must seek", which is false on flash; left at 4 the planner over-prices random I/O and prefers sequential scans where an index scan would win. Now **`1.1`**, the standard SSD value, applied with `ALTER SYSTEM` + `pg_reload_conf()` and confirmed `pending_restart=false` — **no database restart and no outage.** On a 10 MB database this changes nothing measurable today; it is worth doing now because it silently starts mattering as the orders table grows, which is exactly the kind of thing nobody revisits. 🟠 **`shared_buffers` was deliberately left at its 128 MB default**, against the usual "25% of RAM" advice: the whole database is **10 MB**, so it already fits many times over, and raising it would require a PostgreSQL restart to buy nothing. Recorded as a revisit-when-the-database-passes-~500 MB item instead — at roughly 200 orders/day that is several years out.

### Added
- **[docs/resilience-reference.md](docs/resilience-reference.md) — a hand-it-to-someone-else page for the automation, ordered by what a stand-in needs rather than by how the system is built.** Everything on this box that runs unattended was documented in the scripts themselves and in `SETUP-HANDOFF.md`, but neither is usable by someone who is *not* the person who built it: the handoff is a build log ordered by construction sequence, and the scripts explain themselves only to whoever is already reading them. This page opens with **"You got an email. What now?"** — subject line → what actually happened → what to do — because an alert is the only reason a non-builder will ever open it. Then the schedule, the five subsystems in plain language, the keystone risk, the don't-do list, and an honest tested/untested split. **Written for a reader who does not know the vocabulary:** no `AUOptions`, no `LogonType Interactive`, no NSSM in the body — "the machine signs itself in automatically, and Google Drive only runs inside a signed-in session" carries the same constraint without the jargon. Paths and service names stayed, because those get typed. An **appendix maps every plain-English phrase back to the actual script and registry value**, which is the part the markdown version earns over the shared page — the repo reader may have to go and change one. **Three things it deliberately does not contain:** healthchecks ping URLs (anyone holding one can mark your checks failed, and this file is in git — the same rule that applies to every other secret here, stated in the doc itself), the seed PINs, and anything else out of `.env`. **The content ordering is the opinionated part.** The keystone risk gets its own section rather than a bullet: all four scheduled tasks are `LogonType Interactive`, so **changing the `User` account password silently stops the off-site backup mirror and every scheduled job at once** — a single point of failure with a wide blast radius, invisible at the time, and the realistic way this setup breaks a year from now. Also published as a shareable page (same content, `Design.md`'s Modern Minimal applied — near-black band, `shadow-sm` cards, semantic colour only for status, severity stripes reused from the Kitchen Board idiom); the markdown file is the canonical copy and is indexed in [README.md](README.md). 🟠 Noticed while matching the house style and **not** fixed, because it is blocked rather than overlooked: [staff-quick-reference.md](docs/staff-quick-reference.md) still tells staff to open `http://192.168.1.10:3000` — the wrong subnet entirely (this LAN is `192.168.0.x`), the same class of stale placeholder as the printer IPs. It is self-labelled "replace with your shop PC IP", and the real value cannot be written until the static IP is chosen, so it waits on that decision.

### Added
- **The whole healthchecks.io dashboard was audited against the machine's real scheduled tasks — 7 checks, all UP — which confirmed the feature-update test was cleared and surfaced two things the dashboard alone does not show.** The `Dreamy Cafe — Windows feature update` check reads **UP** with `Period 26 weeks / Grace 2 weeks`, so the test `/fail` from earlier was properly cleared by a plain ping. **Every Dreamy period was cross-checked against its actual task trigger rather than trusted**, because a period that disagrees with its schedule false-alarms on a perfectly healthy system: backup cron `30 21 * * *` against `NextRunTime 21:30` ✓, maintenance `4 weeks / 4 days` against `-WeeksInterval 4` ✓, restore drill `13 weeks / 1 week` ✓, feature update `26 weeks / 2 weeks` ✓ — **no mismatches**. 🟠 **First finding: the next feature-update email is real, not a test, and lands in three days.** `Get-ScheduledTaskInfo` puts the reminder's first fire at **`2026-08-02 08:00`** — the `-WeeksInterval 26` start-at-next-matching-Sunday behaviour, not 26 weeks out — so a genuine "down" email arrives days after the clear. Recorded prominently because the obvious reaction is to assume the clear failed and clear it again, and **clearing without doing the version bump is the one action that turns this check into decoration**. (`DreamyCafe Restore Drill` fires `2026-08-03` for the same reason but pings plain, so it makes no noise.) 🟠 **Second finding: a naming hazard the two machines create jointly.** Three of the seven checks are the **old shop PC's** (`DreamyCafe DB Backup` / `DB Maintenance` / `Restore Drill`, last pings 20–21 h ago — that machine is still live and still backing up), and they kept the `DreamyCafe` prefix while only the *checks* on this box were renamed to `Dreamy Cafe … — DREAMY-CAFE`. **This box's scheduled tasks are still named `DreamyCafe *`.** So on cutover day, switching the old PC off makes its three checks go red and email **`DreamyCafe DB Backup is DOWN`** — indistinguishable at a glance from this box's backup task failing, at exactly the moment real failures matter most. The standing "do not delete the old PC's checks while it runs" decision is unchanged; what is added is to **verify the red checks' UUIDs belong to the old machine before investigating**, and only then delete them.

### Fixed
- **🔴 `StorefrontHome.js` is unreachable dead code — no route imports it — so yesterday's "real address and hours on both public pages" was only ever true of one page.** Checking whether the edit was actually live is what surfaced it: the served HTML from the running service returns **200** with `title: Dreamy Cafe POS` and **neither** the address nor the hours in it. Grepping the whole repo for `StorefrontHome` returns its own `export default` and nothing else — every other hit is documentation. [page.js](src/app/page.js) redirects `/` **unconditionally**: LAN to `/pos`, public zone to `/order`, so there is no request path on which the component renders. The component was orphaned at some point by that public-zone redirect — [CHANGELOG line 624](CHANGELOG.md) still describes it as the "Customer homepage at `/` … shown on `dreamy-cafe.com`", which stopped being true when public `/` was pointed at `/order`. **What this does and does not change:** the "hand-synced by design" instruction in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) is now marked half-true — copying between the two files is still the right habit, since `marketing/index.html`'s own comment names `StorefrontHome.js` as its source, but **editing `StorefrontHome.js` changes nothing a customer sees**, and treating that edit as "the storefront is updated" is the specific mistake to avoid. The address and hours *are* correct in `marketing/index.html`, which is the page that will actually be published — but it **has not been deployed**, so they are right in the repo and visible nowhere yet, pending the domain-blocked Cloudflare Pages deploy. **Deliberately not changed:** the file is left in place rather than deleted or wired into a route, because whether the app should serve a marketing homepage at all is a product decision now that `marketing/index.html` is the real published page and the public zone intentionally sends customers straight to `/order`. Also confirmed while checking: the edit *had* been built and deployed correctly (`.next/BUILD_ID` at `20:49:20` against a `19:32:04` source edit, service restarted `20:49:21`) — the change is live, it is simply live in a component nothing reaches, which is why a rebuild check alone would have looked like success.

### Changed
- **Two go-live blockers in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) were stale and are now closed, and re-checking the remaining ones found the staff PIN as well as admin and manager still on its seed default.** The handoff was answered against the live machine rather than read, which is the only reason the drift showed up: **online ordering hours are now enforced** — `OnlineOrderSettings` has its row (`id=1`, `hoursEnabled=true`, `acceptingOrders=true`, written from Admin by the owner), which is what closed the no-row fallback, since [route.js](src/app/api/online-order-settings/route.js) upserts with `create: { id: 1, … }` and the *first save* creates the row as a side effect. The seven day windows still read Mon–Fri `07:00–17:00`, Sat `07:30–15:00`, Sun `08:00–15:00`, unchanged from the real trading hours — so the open question of whether online ordering should close *with* the shop or earlier is answered as **with the shop**, and the thing to watch on opening week is a 16:58 order the kitchen cannot fill by 17:00 (shave `closeTime`, not `hoursEnabled`). The **printer-IP** item was also stale — a second, older entry still claimed `.env` held `192.168.1.100`/`192.168.1.101`, which had already been corrected to `192.168.0.12`/`192.168.0.4`; two entries describing the same thing in opposite states is worse than either alone, so it now points at the fuller one. ⚠ **Recorded while verifying, because it is easy to under-read: the per-day time inputs render `opacity-40 pointer-events-none` while `hoursEnabled` is off** ([page.js:155](src/app/admin/settings/page.js#L155)) — they look broken rather than disabled, which is a support call waiting to happen if anyone ever toggles hours back off. **The PIN check is the new finding.** The seed accounts were assumed to be `admin=0000` / `manager=1234`; testing `bcrypt.compare` against the live hashes confirms **all three** are untouched — `Admin 0000`, `Manager 1234`, and **`Staff 1111`**, which was not previously written down anywhere. All three are hashed, so the defaults are not readable from the database and only a compare would have shown it — meaning "the PINs are still default" was a guess until now.

### Added
- **Orders now carry a short reference number that restarts at 1 each trading day, instead of everything quoting the database primary key.** Nothing was resetting `Order.id` — it is a Postgres `SERIAL`, so the number on every docket, receipt and tracking page only ever climbed, and by year two an order would be `#1487`. ⚠ **One premise behind the original framing was wrong and is corrected here:** this was pitched as "so staff don't shout *order 1487* across the counter", but **dockets are internal working tickets for the baristas and cooks and are never handed to a customer** — in-store customers are called by table or name, which is exactly why `orderLocationLabel` is the docket's headline. So `dailyNumber` is **mainly a staff reference** (it ties the FRONT and KITCHEN copies of one order together and matches a docket back to the POS), and a customer only ever meets it on the **online tracking page** or a **printed receipt**. That correction cost one line of the implementation: the docket's order line had been upgraded to double-size bold on the assumption it was called out, and is now back at the original `tall` size so it does not compete with the location and items — the two lines a cook actually reads. Recorded in [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md) so the next change to that layout starts from the real audience. New `Order.dailyNumber` + `Order.businessDate` (local `YYYY-MM-DD`) and an `OrderDayCounter` table hand out `#1, #2, #3…` per day; `Order.id` is untouched and stays the permanent key. **The two numbers are deliberately not interchangeable, and the split is the whole design**: `id` remains what URLs, React keys, FK joins, the refund cash-movement note and Square reconciliation are built from, while `dailyNumber` is the only thing a customer or a barista ever reads. Display goes through `orderTicketLabel()` / `orderTicketWithId()` ([formatters.js](src/lib/formatters.js)) — dockets, receipts, the tables screen, the `/online` board and the public tracking page show the bare ticket, while **Sales History, Order History, the detail modal and the refund modal show `id` alongside it**, because a ticket number repeats every day and is ambiguous the moment a view spans more than one. Both helpers fall back to `#<id>`, so an unnumbered order can never render a blank label. **Assignment is atomic, not read-then-write.** `nextDailyNumber()` ([orderNumber.js](src/lib/orderNumber.js)) is a single `INSERT .. ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE .. RETURNING` — the `ON CONFLICT` takes a row lock, so a second till waits for the first to commit rather than reading a stale value the way `SELECT max(dailyNumber) + 1` would; two tablets ringing simultaneously is the *normal* case here, not an edge case. A `@@unique([businessDate, dailyNumber])` index sits behind it as belt-and-braces, so calling the same number twice in one day is impossible rather than merely unlikely. **The two paths draw their number at deliberately different moments.** POS draws it **inside the same transaction as the insert**, so a card-verify rejection or a lost idempotency race hands the number back and the day's sequence stays gapless — a real difference from `id`, which burns a value on every rolled-back transaction. Online orders draw it in `fulfillPaidOrder` **at payment, not at checkout creation**: online orders are created `AWAITING_PAYMENT` and abandoned checkouts are swept, so numbering at creation would have riddled the day with holes for orders that never existed, and it also correctly dates a ticket by the day it is *made* rather than the day someone opened a payment page. That ordering has a consequence worth knowing — an `AWAITING_PAYMENT` order has **no** ticket number, which is why the Stripe/Square `paymentNote` in [onlineCheckout.js](src/lib/onlineCheckout.js) still uses the id and must keep doing so. Numbering there is also **non-fatal by design** (logged, never thrown): it runs after the exactly-once status-flip claim, and a DB hiccup must not cost the customer their loyalty stamps or leave a paid order sitting unprinted when the fallback is simply a docket headed by its id. **The timezone trap is the part most likely to be reintroduced.** `createdAt` is stored in UTC, so grouping orders into trading days with `createdAt::date` files every order rung after 10am AEST under the *next* day — which is why day keys are computed in JS via `localDayKey` and why [backfill-daily-numbers.js](prisma/backfill-daily-numbers.js) is a Node script rather than one clever `UPDATE`. The backfill is optional (old orders already fall back to their id), re-runnable, resumes each day from its counter, and skips `AWAITING_PAYMENT` rows. Two smaller follow-ons: searching Sales History by a number now matches **either** the ticket or the id within the selected date range (and tolerates a typed `#`), since staff read tickets off dockets; and the accounting CSV gains a `ticket` column after `orderId` — additive, but it does shift later columns, so any spreadsheet keyed on column *position* wants re-checking. The thermal receipt keeps both, as `Order #12` with `(ref 1487)` on the date line, in ASCII like every other printed line. **The counter was proven against the live database rather than reasoned about**, because "atomic" is the one claim here that fails silently if wrong: on a throwaway `1999-01-01` day, sequential draws returned `1, 2, 3, 4`; **five concurrent draws returned five distinct numbers**; a deliberately rolled-back transaction drew `11` and the next draw was **`11` again**, confirming the number is genuinely released rather than burned; and a second day started independently at `1`. Test rows deleted, `OrderDayCounter` left empty. The migration is applied (`20260730000000_add_daily_order_numbers` — both columns nullable, unique index `Order_businessDate_dailyNumber_key` present), which is safe to do ahead of the code because it is purely additive and the running build simply ignores columns it does not know about. **Deployed and live**: service stopped → `prisma generate` → `npm run build` → service started → `/pos` returns **200**. The stop is not optional and is now recorded in [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md): `prisma generate` fails `EPERM: … rename query_engine-windows.dll.node` while `DreamyCafeApp` holds the engine DLL, because Windows will not let a mapped image be replaced — and **killing Node is not enough, since NSSM restarts it**. 🟠 Worth flagging as a latent trap in the deploy path itself: [deploy.ps1](scripts/deploy.ps1) builds at step 4 **while the service is still running** (deliberately, so a failed build never restarts the app), which means `npm run build` inside it will hit this same EPERM on *any* schema change. Left unmodified rather than reworked unasked. **The create path was then exercised for real and rolled back**: the regenerated client accepted `businessDate`/`dailyNumber`, the `$transaction` wrapper and nested `items.create` worked, and the order came out `id 1, dailyNumber 1, businessDate 2026-07-30` — rendering as **`#1`**. ⚠ **The rollback needed a second step that is easy to miss**: Postgres sequences do **not** roll back, so a throwaway insert would have quietly consumed `Order.id = 1` and made the shop's first genuine sale `#2`, undoing the sequence reset done when the pre-opening test orders were cleared. `Order_id_seq` and `OrderItem_id_seq` were captured before and `setval`-restored after, and both re-read `last_value 1, is_called false`; order count back to `0`, `OrderDayCounter` back to `0` rows. **Also verified:** schema validates, all tests pass, and every edited file parses. **Still unverified:** nothing has been printed — the ticket number's new `big`/`bold` docket line is untested against the physical printer.

### Fixed
- **The Print Queue was showing the database id where the reprinted docket shows the ticket number — the one screen where a mismatch is most expensive.** [listFailedDockets](src/lib/printQueue.js) was the only order read left with a narrow `select` that had not been widened, so it could not see `dailyNumber`, and [PrintQueueModal.js](src/components/shared/PrintQueueModal.js) rendered a raw `#{job.orderId}`. Staff recovering a docket that failed to print would read **`#1487`** in the queue, hit Reprint, and get paper headed **`Order #12`** — two different numbers for one order, at the exact moment someone is trying to match a lost docket to a waiting customer. The sibling read used by the reprint itself (`getPrintJob`) uses `include:`, so it always had the number; only the *list* was blind. Now carries `dailyNumber` and renders `orderTicketWithId` → **`#12 · id 1487`**, using the id-bearing variant deliberately: failed jobs persist in the queue until reprinted or dismissed, so the list genuinely spans days. **Found by auditing every sibling `Order` read rather than by testing the feature** — of the ~15 order queries across the app, all but two use `include:` (which returns every scalar and so picked up the new column for free); the two narrow `select`s were the public track route (fixed with the feature) and this one, which was missed. `PrintQueueWatcher` and `/api/print-queue` were checked and display no order number. Also added a check that the accounting CSV keeps `ticket` and the money columns in their own cells, since that column was inserted mid-row and a header/row mismatch would silently shift every money value one column right in an accountant's spreadsheet. **The concurrency claim was re-tested at the level that actually matters**: eight *committing interactive transactions* firing at once — the real two-tills-at-checkout path, not the non-transactional draws tested earlier — returned `#1…#8`, all distinct, all contiguous, **zero deadlocks and zero transaction timeouts**, with the counter finishing at exactly `8`. The wait times make the design visible and name its ceiling: `74ms, 130ms, 199ms, 262ms, 324ms, 380ms, 439ms, 504ms` — each checkout **queues behind the ones ahead of it on the counter row**, so the number is never duplicated but concurrent checkouts do serialise. Fine at two tills against a sub-50ms insert; it is the one figure to revisit if this shop ever runs many tills at once. Counter-only, so no `Order` rows were created and no id sequence moved. 🟠 **Reported rather than changed: the cashier never sees the number on screen.** The POS success toast reads only `Order placed! Change: $X.XX`, so in-store the number exists solely on the printed docket. ⚠ **This was initially written up as more serious than it is** — the concern was "with the printer offline staff have no number to call out", which assumed a called-out number; in-store customers are called by table or name, so the real cost is only that a cashier cannot quote the reference without the paper. Left alone: surfacing it needs `postOrderWithRetry` to hand its parsed response body up through `saveOrderVisibly`, which is the money-critical save/retry path, and that is not worth touching for a minor convenience.
- **The real address and trading hours are now on both public pages — and chasing the hours through the system found online ordering would have accepted orders at 3am.** [marketing/index.html](marketing/index.html) and [StorefrontHome.js](src/components/public/StorefrontHome.js) both carried `123 Main Street, Your Suburb, NSW 2000` and invented hours; both now read **12 Example Street, Sampleton, ACT 2600** (from `RECEIPT_ADDRESS`) and **Mon–Fri 7:00 am – 5:00 pm, Sat 7:30 am – 3:00 pm, Sun 8:00 am – 3:00 pm**. Note the placeholder was **NSW 2000** against a real **ACT 2600** — the state was wrong, not just the street, which is the kind of edit that looks done after fixing one line. The two files are **hand-synced by design** (the marketing page is a separate Cloudflare Pages deploy and shares no code with the app), so both were edited together or the LAN preview would have drifted from the public site. **The functional half is the part that mattered more.** `OnlineOrderHours` held **generic `07:00–18:00` on all seven days** — not the shop's hours, and wrong in both directions: an hour too late on weekdays, three hours too late at weekends, and an hour too early on Sunday. Now set per-day to the real windows, with a check that none of them accidentally satisfy `closeTime <= openTime`, which the schema interprets as an **overnight window** and would have turned a typo into a 21-hour trading day. 🔴 **But they are not being enforced, and the reason is a bug pattern this project has now hit twice.** `OnlineOrderSettings` **has no row at all**, so `hoursEnabled` falls back to **`false`** — meaning online ordering currently accepts orders at **any hour of any day**, including while the shop is shut and nobody is there to make them. This is exactly the failure that put both payment channels on Stripe: a settings table whose `@default(...)` never applied because **a column default only takes effect when a row is inserted**, and the seed never inserts one, so the code's own fallback silently governs instead. Recorded as a go-live blocker in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) needing one human decision — whether online ordering should close *with* the shop or earlier, so the last order can actually be made — rather than being switched on unilaterally, since enabling it changes customer-facing behaviour. 🟠 Still fake on both pages: the **phone number** (`(02) 1234 5678`), because `RECEIPT_PHONE` is still `CHANGE_ME` and there is no real number to use; the hours block that sat beside it was the only part that could be made true.

### Fixed
- **The twice-yearly Windows feature-update reminder could not reach anybody, and testing it is the only reason that surfaced.** Triggering the task manually proved the mechanics were fine — `result=0`, log written, and the message correctly reads the **live pinned version from the registry** (`25H2`) rather than a hardcoded string. But it only *appeared* to work because the run happened while someone was sitting in an active RDP session. Its two output channels were a local log file nobody reads and an `msg.exe` popup, and this box is deliberately built to run **24/7 headless behind autologon + immediate lock** — so on the day it actually fires, six months out, there is nobody signed in, the popup lands on a locked screen, and the log line is never seen. Every sibling task (`backup`, `maintenance`, `restore drill`) pings healthchecks.io; this one pinged nothing. That matters because of what it guards: the Windows feature version is pinned so the semi-annual upgrade cannot install itself mid-trade, and **a pin left alone eventually falls out of servicing and stops receiving security patches** — this reminder is the only thing between "deliberately pinned" and "silently unsupported". [feature-update-reminder.ps1](scripts/feature-update-reminder.ps1) now pings `FEATURE_UPDATE_HEALTHCHECK_URL`. **Two design choices worth stating, because both are the reverse of the repo's other checks.** It pings **`/fail`, not the plain URL** — every other check here is a dead-man's switch that alarms when pings *stop*, whereas this one exists to *tell a human to act*, and a plain ping means "up" and sends no notification at all; `/fail` alerts immediately and carries the message body. It also leaves the dashboard honest, since the check stays **DOWN while the update is still outstanding** and is cleared by pinging the plain URL once the update is done and re-pinned. And there is **deliberately no fallback to `BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL`**, unlike `db-maintenance.ps1`: because this script pings `/fail`, a shared check would announce a **backup failure that never happened**, on the one check that has to stay trustworthy. With the key unset the reminder still runs and records `NO ALERT SENT — … reached nobody off this machine` rather than skipping in silence; a ping that fails is caught and logged too, since a reminder that crashes is worse than one that only managed to write its log. **Verified end-to-end 2026-07-30:** with the key set, the task logged `Alert sent (healthchecks.io /fail)` at `19:06:49` with `result=0` and the alert email arrived — so the reminder now genuinely leaves the machine, which was the entire point. (The email reads "down"; that is the mechanism working, since down means *action outstanding*.) Documented in [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) alongside the other three, with the period/grace table extended. **The check's schedule is `Simple`, `Period 26 weeks`, `Grace 14 days` — deliberately not `OnCalendar`:** the task trigger is `-Weekly -WeeksInterval 26`, a **182-day rolling interval rather than a calendar half-year**, so it drifts earlier through the year (the same trap already documented for the 4-weekly maintenance task) and calendar dates would diverge from it a little each cycle until a healthy system produced false "late" alerts. Sizing the period properly also earns a **second, independent signal** instead of noise: the `/fail` ping says the reminder *fired*, while the period expiring says it **never fired at all** — the scheduled task lost in a rebuild, or the box switched off for months. The dashboard defaults (`Period 1 day`, `Grace 1 hour`) are called out as wrong here, since they would false-alarm a day after every time the check is cleared.

### Changed
- **The demo seed menu is replaced with the shop's real menu, recovered from the old PC's backup without going to the shop — and verified field-by-field rather than eyeballed.** Was 4 categories / 15 generic demo products (`Americano`, `Bubble Tea`, `Avocado Toast`…); now **8 categories, 29 products, 9 sizes, 27 product modifiers, 16 category modifiers and 1 combo** — Coffee, Tea, Food, Cold Drinks, Cake, Combos, Sandwiches, Breakfast, including `Breakfast Deals` at $12.00 with `Coffee` and `Food` choose-from slots. Extracted exactly as the printer IPs were: old dump → **throwaway database** → JSON → dropped, with **nothing restored into `dreamycafe`**, so the fresh-shop decision still holds — this is a menu import, not a migration, and no orders, customers, staff, sessions or settings came across. **Three decisions in this that were not arbitrary.** *Original ids were preserved*, because `ComboSlotOption` references products **by id** and remapping them would have silently rewired the combo to the wrong items — a failure that shows up as a customer receiving the wrong food, not as an error; the id sequences were then realigned to `max(id)+1` so the next product added from the Admin UI cannot collide on the primary key. *Prices were cast to `text` in the extract query and inserted as decimal strings*, never as JSON numbers, because a JSON number would have round-tripped every price through a binary float — the exact thing this repo's money rule exists to prevent. *The whole import was verified by diffing every row against the source* — name, price, category, printer routing, loyalty flag, availability, stock — with prices compared as exact decimal strings: **zero mismatches, zero leftovers, zero orphans**, and the combo re-resolves to real products. ⚠ **`npm run db:seed` was then checked rather than assumed safe**, since it would be the obvious way to undo all of this: it upserts products by hardcoded id `1..15` with `update: {}`, and there are **no gaps in 1..15**, so every upsert is a no-op; all four seed category names already exist; and `printerConfig.createMany` uses `skipDuplicates` while now reading the corrected `PRINTER_*_IP` values. Re-seeding therefore cannot re-inject demo items — **but only while ids 1–15 stay occupied**, so deleting products down into that range would make a later `db:seed` backfill the gaps with demo products. 🟠 Two typos were carried over **verbatim rather than silently corrected** — `Egg Benedic` and `Chli Sauce` (spelled `Chili Sauce` on the sibling item) — so the import is an honest copy and the owner decides; likewise the prices are the old shop's **as of the 29 July backup** and want confirming before opening.

### Fixed
- **The wrong-subnet printer IPs are corrected with the shop's real addresses, recovered from the old PC without going to the shop — and the kitchen printer, which nobody knew the address of, turned out to be `192.168.0.4`.** `PrinterConfig` held seed defaults `FRONT = 192.168.1.100` / `KITCHEN = 192.168.1.101` on `192.168.1.x` against a shop LAN of `192.168.0.x`; dockets would have failed as **timeouts** rather than as anything that reads like a misconfiguration. Now `FRONT = 192.168.0.12` and `KITCHEN = 192.168.0.4`, port `9100`, both `GENERIC`/80 mm, receipts on `FRONT` only — matching the old shop exactly. **The retrieval method is the reusable part:** the old PC still backs up nightly to the same Google Drive account, so its dump was restored into a **throwaway database**, read, and dropped. ⚠ **This is not a migration and does not soften the fresh-shop decision** — nothing was restored into `dreamycafe`; one config table was read from a scratch copy that was then destroyed, along with the local copy of the dump. ⚠ **Two traps worth recording for anyone repeating it:** the old PC's dumps live in the **shared root** of `DreamyCafe-Backups`, not the per-machine `DREAMY-CAFE\` subfolder; and `pg_restore` **fails outright on a dump read directly from `G:`** with `could not read input file: Invalid argument`, because Google Drive for Desktop serves a streamed virtual file while `pg_restore` needs seekable random access — copy it to local disk first. **`.env` carried the same two wrong addresses as fallbacks** (`PRINTER_FRONT_IP` / `PRINTER_KITCHEN_IP`), which `resolvePrinterConfig` ([printer.js:144](src/lib/printer.js#L144)) substitutes whenever a DB `ip` is blank — so fixing only the database would have left a landmine that reappears the instant someone clears a field. Both are now correct. A blank IP, for the record, is the *safe* failure: [printer.js:59](src/lib/printer.js#L59) rejects immediately with `Printer IP not configured` rather than hanging. Neither address answers from home, and nothing else on the home LAN squats on them, so the config is unambiguous — but a printer re-addressed by DHCP since the last backup would not show up here, so one physical confirmation is still owed.
- **Pre-opening test orders cleared, id sequences reset, after a verified backup.** Four orders (three card tests plus the live `$3.50` Square charge that was refunded, `pay:3jAMh5JTelwJgrSR0zR8X7QEOseZY`) would otherwise have landed in the first real Z-report. `Order` children cascade cleanly — `OrderItem` → `OrderItemModifier`, and `PrintJob` — so nothing was orphaned, and the `Order`/`OrderItem` id sequences were restarted so the first genuine sale is `#1`. A full backup was taken and verified first. ⚠ **Square keeps its own record of that charge and refund** — deleting the local row does not and should not alter Square's ledger, so `reconcile-square.js` will show it as unmatched on Square's side. That is correct behaviour, not a defect.

### Added
- **Reboot survival is now proven the hard way: the owner pulled the power cord and the box came back on its own.** Boot `18:18:54`, **app started 0.5 min after boot**, every resilience check passing. This is a materially stronger claim than the clean-reboot proof recorded earlier the same day, because a dirty power cut is the only test that actually exercises the BIOS **After Power Loss → Power On** rule, **PostgreSQL's crash recovery**, and the `DependOnService` ordering that stops `prisma migrate deploy` racing the database on the way up — a graceful `Restart-Computer` exercises none of them. It is also the exact failure this machine was specified around, given the deliberate decision not to buy a UPS. **The ethernet cable went in at the same time**, which unblocks the last genuinely-blocked home task: `Ethernet 2` is now **Up at 1 Gbps holding `192.168.0.22/24`**, so the adapter that will be used at the shop is the live one and the static IP can finally be set on the right interface — a static IP being per-adapter, setting it on the Wi-Fi dongle would have bought nothing. ⚠ Recorded with it: **`.12` and `.4` are now taken by the printers**, and `.22` is a DHCP *lease* rather than a reservation, so it is not safe to simply keep.

### Fixed
- **Square is now end-to-end ready — `verify-square-production-ready.js` returns `PASS` on every line** — and a state audit taken at the same time found a **printer misconfiguration that would have failed on opening day**. The reader is saved (`COUNTER`, enabled, `squareDeviceId=538CS145C4000655`), both provider channels read `SQUARE`, and all four Square environment variables are set, so the only payments step left is a real tap — which is **live money** at `SQUARE_ENV=production` and should be refunded after. **The printer finding is the more valuable half.** `PrinterConfig` still holds the seed defaults `FRONT = 192.168.1.100` and `KITCHEN = 192.168.1.101` — on **`192.168.1.x`**, while this shop's LAN is **`192.168.0.x`**. That is not a near-miss to tidy up on the day: they are addresses on a network that does not exist at either site, so dockets would fail as **timeouts** rather than as anything that reads like a configuration error, at the moment the first customer is waiting. It also partly overturns the standing "printer IPs are irreducibly shop-only" classification recorded in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md): the **subnet** can be corrected at home, and the old machine's logs put the front printer at **`192.168.0.12`**, so `FRONT` may need no shop-side change at all — leaving only the kitchen printer's final octet genuinely unknown. ⚠ `192.168.0.12` comes from log archaeology on the still-running old PC, not from a live reading, so it is recorded as needing confirmation against the physical printer rather than as fact. Also noted in passing: the database still carries **4 test orders** and the **demo seed menu** (4 categories, 15 generic products) plus the three default seed accounts, all of which are go-live content tasks rather than configuration.

### Fixed
- **The STRIPE-fallback payment bug is confirmed closed, and the readiness script that missed half of it now checks both channels.** The `PaymentSettings` row exists and reads `provider: SQUARE`, `onlineProvider: SQUARE` (`updatedAt 2026-07-30T07:15:01Z`) — verified by direct query rather than by the UI appearing to save, since the original bug was precisely that the runtime value and the apparent configuration disagreed. Two things were checked that could each have made the fix real-but-not-in-effect: `getPaymentSettings()` reads the database **per request**, so no restart was needed for the provider switch; and `.env` was last written at 00:47 against a service process that started at 16:35, so the running server's Square credentials are current too — a `.env` edited *after* the service start would have left the box verifying one set of credentials from the CLI while serving another. **The script gap is the part worth fixing rather than just noting.** [verify-square-production-ready.js](scripts/verify-square-production-ready.js) only ever asserted on `settings?.provider` — the **in-store** channel — so the online channel, a separate `onlineProvider` column, was never reported at all. That is the same blind spot that let a whole channel sit on the wrong provider unnoticed, embedded in the very tool meant to catch it. It now mirrors the real fallback chain from [paymentSettings.js](src/lib/paymentSettings.js) so it reports **what actually runs rather than what the schema declares**, and it grades the result by consequence instead of by preference: online on Square is `OK`; online on Stripe with a **live** key is `OK` and labelled a deliberate in-store/online split, since that is a legitimate configuration the docs support; online on Stripe with an **`sk_test…`** key is a `FIX`, because that is the money-losing case — `/order` card payments succeed, capture nothing, and look completely normal. Both channels now report `OK`.

### Added
- **A read-only audit of the Square account answered the question that was actually blocking Terminal pairing: whether re-pairing would break the still-running old shop PC.** It would not, and the evidence is specific — the account holds **four `PAIRED` `TERMINAL_API` device codes** (`GZPSSH`, `NXJYZC`, `KBKEMX`, `EDRGEX`, created 7 and 11 July) and **all four point at the same device id, `538CS145C4000655`**. So device codes **coexist rather than supersede**, and the device id is the *hardware's* identity, not a per-install lease: generating another code cannot unpair anything. The hardware itself is `name="Dreamy Cafe"`, `Square Terminal (1st Gen, v2)`, firmware `5.65.0084`, status **`AVAILABLE`** at location `S02XD1CK7H3QX`. This also makes pairing **optional** — pasting `538CS145C4000655` into **Square Device ID** is equivalent, since that pairing is already live for this application and location, and [square-terminal-setup.md](docs/square-terminal-setup.md) §5 already sanctions pasting the id. ⚠ The `device:` prefix the Devices API returns (`device:538CS145C4000655`) is **not** part of the value; the Terminal API wants the bare id, and the two endpoints reporting the same thing in two formats is an easy way to save a value that never works. Pairing's token permission (`DEVICE_CREDENTIAL_MANAGEMENT`) is already proven in passing, since listing device codes requires it and the call succeeded against production. 🟠 **One hazard was recorded rather than resolved:** a device id is a stored value with **no exclusivity**, so once this machine has it, *both* it and the old shop PC can drive the same Terminal — structurally the same failure as two machines sharing one named Cloudflare tunnel, and just as silent. Harmless while the Terminal is at home; on cutover day the old PC has to be **stopped**, not merely left alone. Recorded in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md), where the reader item is also reclassified from "shop-side" to a **home** task — the Terminal reaches Square over the internet, not the shop LAN.

### Fixed
- **🔴 The first real reboot test of `DreamyCafeApp` printed `[OK] App service started AT BOOT (unattended)` off a garbage number, so the reboot-survival claim it was written to prove is still unproven.** The script computed the delta between the app process's start time and `LastBootUpTime` and printed `boot 2026-07-30 16:35:31, uptime 2.6 min, app started -1,065,350,435.5 min after boot` — roughly **negative 2,026 years** — and then passed it, because the check was `if ($deltaMin -le 5)`, which is true for any sufficiently negative number as much as for a genuinely small one. **Root cause:** [verify-resilience.ps1](scripts/verify-resilience.ps1) read the app's start time with `Get-Process -Id $svcPid` — but `Get-Process`'s `.StartTime` needs a process handle opened with `PROCESS_QUERY_(LIMITED_)INFORMATION`, and a **non-elevated** session (no `Administrator:` in the prompt title in the reported run) can be denied that against a `SYSTEM`-owned NSSM process. The failed read didn't throw or show a visible error — it silently came back `$null`, and PowerShell's `$null - $boot` coerced `$null` to `[datetime]::MinValue` (year 0001) rather than erroring, and `(0001-01-01 - 2026-07-30).TotalMinutes` is exactly the ~2,026-year negative number observed. **Fixed** by reading the start time through `Get-CimInstance Win32_Process … .CreationDate` instead of `Get-Process`/`.StartTime` — CIM asks the WMI service to look the process up rather than opening a handle to it directly, so it works the same unelevated as elevated. **Also added a sanity guard that was missing regardless of which API is used**: a delta more than ~1 minute before boot, or later than "now", is now reported `[SKIP] … delta (…) is out of range for this boot - re-run to confirm` rather than being evaluated by the pass/fail thresholds at all — a script should never let an impossible number produce a confident-looking `[OK]`. **Re-verified against the same boot session with the fix in place: `app started 0.2 min after boot` against `uptime 32.5 min`** — the app came up within ~12 seconds of the machine itself, which is what actually proves NSSM's `DreamyCafeApp` starts unattended rather than merely being `Running` when someone checks. The false positive was caught from the user's own screenshot of the real run, not from a code review; the fix was verified the same way.

### Fixed
- **🔴 The shop is currently pointed at STRIPE on both channels with a `sk_test…` key — by omission, not by choice — and yesterday's changelog entry asserting the opposite was wrong.** That entry called the test key "harmless while Square is the active provider on both channels"; the premise was never checked. Running [verify-square-production-ready.js](scripts/verify-square-production-ready.js) reported `In-store provider is STRIPE`, and a direct query explains it: the **`PaymentSettings` table is empty — there is no row at all.** [paymentSettings.js:19-20](src/lib/paymentSettings.js#L19-L20) then resolves `provider: settings?.provider || 'STRIPE'` and `onlineProvider: settings?.onlineProvider || settings?.provider || 'STRIPE'`, so a missing row means **both** channels fall back to Stripe in code. The schema is actively misleading on this point: `PaymentSettings.provider` carries `@default("SQUARE")`, but **a column default only applies when a row is inserted**, and `npm run db:seed` never inserts one — so reading the schema tells you the opposite of the runtime truth, which is presumably how it went unnoticed. **Consequence had it gone live:** card charges in-person *and* online run in Stripe **test mode** and capture **no real money**, while failing in the most expensive way available — Stripe accepts the test charge, so the POS reports success, the docket prints, and the customer leaves unbilled. Nothing looks broken. **Fix is one human action:** Admin → Payments → set provider to **SQUARE** for both channels, which writes the row; then re-run the readiness script and require **OK** rather than `FIX` on the in-store line, *before* pairing the Terminal. Recorded as the top payments blocker in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md), replacing the incorrect "closed, harmless" note. The reverse warning still holds: never switch *to* Stripe while that key is `sk_test…`. No code changed — the STRIPE fallback is left exactly as it is, since altering payment routing to paper over a missing data row would be the wrong repair.

### Fixed
- **Running the POS as a service silently broke the service-worker cache key, and it would have forced the iPad till to re-download the whole app on every restart — including the 04:00 update reboot.** NSSM runs `DreamyCafeApp` as `NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM`, and git refuses a repository owned by another account: `fatal: detected dubious ownership`. `npm run start` fires `prestart` → [bump-sw-cache.js](scripts/bump-sw-cache.js), whose `git rev-parse --short HEAD` therefore threw and fell through to `buildId = Date.now().toString(36)`. That defeats the exact invariant the script's own comment documents — keying on the commit id so an unchanged HEAD regenerates a **byte-identical** `sw.js` and tablets are *not* needlessly re-versioned. **Confirmed by observation rather than inference:** `public/sw.js` read `dc-pos-0.1.0-90064e4` after a manual `npm start`, and `dc-pos-0.1.0-ms742k0g` — a base-36 timestamp — after the service started, against HEAD `90064e4`. So every service start minted a new cache version, and the service restarts on crash (`AppExit Restart`) and at every reboot. The failure would have surfaced as the till reloading itself at 4am on a shop connection, i.e. as a blank screen at opening. Fixed at the machine level with `git config --system --add safe.directory C:/Users/User/Desktop/DreamyCafe` — system scope, so it covers SYSTEM as well as `User` — rather than by weakening the script's fallback, which is a correct last resort for a genuine non-git deployment. **Verified by restarting the service and re-reading the file: `dc-pos-0.1.0-90064e4`, matching HEAD, with a clean stderr.** Worth knowing: `public/sw.js` is gitignored (`.gitignore:17`), so the rewrite-on-every-boot never dirtied the working tree — which is also why nothing surfaced this earlier. The only remaining stderr output is Prisma's `5.22.0 -> 7.9.1` upgrade notice, cosmetic and deliberately deferred until after opening.

### Added
- **`install-windows-services.ps1` takes `-AppOnly`, which moves reboot survival (Phase 10) from shop-fitting day to home.** The script was all-or-nothing in a way that gated the half with no shop dependency behind the half that has nothing but: it resolved `cloudflared` at line 23 and threw on a missing `config.yml` **before installing anything**, so the POS app service — which just wraps `npm run start` against local PostgreSQL — could not be installed until a domain existed, a tunnel was created, and a Cloudflare Transform Rule was in place. `-AppOnly` installs `DreamyCafeApp` alone; re-running without the switch later adds the tunnel cleanly. The reason this is worth doing early rather than efficiently: **proving a service survives a cold boot costs several reboots of wall-clock time**, and that is the worst kind of debt to still owe with a shop to fit out. Three details were handled rather than assumed: the teardown loop now removes **only the services this run reinstalls**, so an `-AppOnly` re-run to update the app can never be the thing that takes the shop's online ordering offline; `install-windows-services.bat` forwards `%*` **through its UAC re-elevation**, which previously dropped all arguments and would have silently reinstated the tunnel path and failed on the missing config; and the closing hint no longer tells this deployment to launch the Electron till (`open-pos.bat`), since the iPad is the till here and `desktop/` is deliberately not installed — it now points at the LAN URL and warns that `start-pos.bat` would collide with the service on port 3000, a failure that is invisible because that `.bat` relaunches hidden through `start-pos-hidden.vbs` and only writes `logs\startup.log`. Verified by AST parse; not yet executed (needs Administrator).
- **The shop-only list in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) was re-audited and split by *why* each item is blocked, because three different reasons were collapsed into one heading.** Items now sort into *irreducibly physical* (printer IPs, live card charge, mounting, off-LAN RDP re-test — different router and NAT, so the home result does not transfer), *blocked on the domain but location-independent* (Transform Rule, tunnel creation, Pages deploy, Square webhook URL — all browser work), and *reclassified as home-doable*: the NSSM app service, **Square Terminal pairing** (needs the device plus internet, not the shop LAN — and the device is at home), the **Ethernet static IP** (the shop LAN is the same `192.168.0.x` range as home), Caddy's server side, and all content/secret entry. Two traps recorded with the reclassification: a static IP is **per-adapter**, so setting it on the home Wi-Fi dongle buys nothing for a box that runs on wired ethernet at the shop — the cable has to go in at home for that work to count; and a LAN TLS certificate must match **how the iPad addresses the POS**, so the address has to be settled *before* the cert is issued or it breaks on the move. Also noted: **starting** the tunnel is constrained only by the old PC possibly running a tunnel named `dreamycafe` (two machines on one named tunnel silently split online orders), and registering the new one under a different name removes that constraint entirely — the name is hardcoded at `install-windows-services.ps1:158`.

### Changed
- **The marketing site is rebranded to Dreamy Cafe, and the domain it links to is now a placeholder that fails visibly instead of a stale one that fails silently.** `marketing/` was the last DreamyCafe-branded thing in the repo and the most public — a separate Cloudflare Pages deploy, so it was outside the app rebrand's build verification entirely. All seven brand strings in [marketing/index.html](marketing/index.html) now read **Dreamy Cafe** (title, meta description, header, hero, about, footer, copyright), and the header tagline is **`Coffee, Pastries & Food`** — read out of `NEXT_PUBLIC_BUSINESS_TAGLINE` rather than invented, because the file's own comment commits its copy to staying in sync with [StorefrontHome.js](src/components/public/StorefrontHome.js), which renders `BRAND_TAGLINE`. The page had been showing `Coffee & Food`, which is [brand.js](src/lib/brand.js)'s *fallback* value, not the configured one — so the static page and the app had already drifted. **The domain change is the load-bearing part.** `DREAMYCAFE_ORDER_ORIGIN` in [marketing/config.js](marketing/config.js) went from `https://dreamy-cafe.com` to **`https://CHANGE_ME.invalid`** (the variable *name* stays — [rebrand-checklist.md](docs/rebrand-checklist.md) §4 — only the value changes). Every **Order Online** and **Loyalty** button on the public page is built from that single value by `dcUrl()`, so shipping the old brand's domain would not have produced a broken page: it would have produced a *working* page pointing real customers at a hostname the shop may not own, with our own site as the referrer, for whoever registers it next. `.invalid` is a reserved TLD (RFC 2606) that can never resolve **or be registered**, so an accidental deploy now yields obviously dead buttons instead. `marketing/_redirects` was de-branded the same way, with a note that it must be filled in *together* with `config.js` since the two describe one DNS split (apex → tunnel, `www` → Pages). ⚠ **What could not be established from the repo:** whether `dreamy-cafe.com` is still owned. [marketing-pages.md](docs/marketing-pages.md) describes it in the present tense, but there is no `cloudflared` config on this box, no domain key in `.env`, and every domain line in [go-live-checklist.md](docs/go-live-checklist.md) is unticked — nothing is deployed, so the placeholder breaks nothing, and restoring a real domain is one line. `marketing-pages.md` also gained a header warning that all ~40 `dreamy-cafe.com` occurrences in it are stand-ins rather than live hostnames; the concrete name was kept in the DNS/tunnel examples on purpose, since those steps are much easier to follow against a real-looking host than against `<domain>`. 🟠 **Deliberately not touched: the "Visit us" block is still fake** — `123 Main Street, Your Suburb NSW 2000`, `(02) 1234 5678`, guessed hours. Filling those with plausible-looking values would be worse than leaving them obviously unset, because a wrong address on a public page sends customers to the wrong building. Recorded in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) as a content task, with the warning that `index.html` and `StorefrontHome.js` are **hand-synced** — editing one diverges the LAN preview from the public site.
- **Domain purchase deferred to just before go-live (owner's decision), with the registration/delegation split recorded because only one half of it is safely deferrable.** The `.invalid` placeholder in [marketing/config.js](marketing/config.js) is built for precisely this wait, so nothing is blocked. What [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) now spells out is that "buying a domain" is two independent actions with opposite timing profiles: **registration** is instant, ~$10–20/yr, needs no configuration, and settles the one choice that genuinely cannot be redone later — the *name*, which stays takeable by someone else every day it is unregistered; **delegation** (nameservers → Cloudflare, apex/`www` records) carries an unavoidable multi-hour external propagation wait and rightly belongs at the end. Register early, delegate late, and the step nobody can hurry stays off the critical path. Also recorded: the domain is a **fan-out dependency rather than a single field** — it lands in `marketing/config.js` *and* `marketing/_redirects`, the tunnel hostname, the **Transform Rule** carrying `PUBLIC_ZONE_SECRET` (which must exist **before** the tunnel starts or internet traffic is treated as LAN), the Square dashboard webhook URL, the `/privacy` page URL plus its marketing-footer link, and the UptimeRobot target. Budget a session, not an edit.
- **BitLocker: decided against, and the decision closes a footgun as well as declining a protection.** The owner assessed physical theft as not credible for this location, and theft — plus eventual disposal — is the entire scope of what disk encryption covers: it does nothing about malware, a stolen RDP password, or anyone who can log into Windows, because the volume decrypts transparently once booted. What stays real when the threat does not is the cost: **the TPM withholds the key whenever the measured boot state changes** — a BIOS/firmware update on this Q22 v02.29.01 board being the everyday trigger — and without the 48-digit recovery key at that moment the disk is **permanently unreadable**, with no vendor backdoor. Declining it removes that failure mode from a box that is otherwise deliberately built to recover unattended. ⚠ **The uncovered case was recorded rather than dropped**: encryption's other job is making retirement "destroy the key" instead of "wipe the drive", so [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) now carries a disposal requirement — if this machine is ever sold, donated or binned, the NVMe must be securely erased or destroyed, because it holds plaintext order history, staff PINs, the Tailscale node key and `.env` with a live Square **production** access token. The re-enable recipe was kept with it (TPM-only, **never** a pre-boot PIN — that would hang the unattended 04:00 update reboot at a prompt with nobody present, losing the `G:` mount and therefore the off-site backup; recovery key stored off-machine *before* encrypting; verify `manage-bde -status C:` lists **both** `TPM` and `Numerical Password`), so a future decision starts from the measured facts instead of re-deriving them.
- **Two further setup decisions are now closed rather than pending, which matters because an undecided item and a decided-against item look identical on a checklist.** **BIOS lockdown (USB/PXE boot disable + BIOS Administrator password) — decided against**: the mini PC sits in a **staff-only zone, not visible to customers**, which is exactly the "back room ⇒ skip both" branch of the rule already recorded. The reasoning is retained in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) rather than deleted, with the condition that would reopen it — the box physically moving somewhere a customer can reach. **`STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` staying a `sk_test…` key — deliberate**: Square is the chosen provider on both channels and Stripe is not in use, so the placeholder stands until Stripe is ever adopted. The residual trap was kept visible instead of being closed away with the item: switching the provider at **Admin → Payments** with a test key present makes card charges run in **test mode and capture no real money**, and it fails *reassuringly* — Stripe accepts the charge, the POS shows success, and nothing arrives. The note now says to swap the key **before** touching that setting.

### Added
- **The elevated pass is done: all five scheduled tasks are registered and four are proven by a real scheduler-triggered run, not by registration succeeding.** `DreamyCafe DB Maintenance` (every 4 weeks, Sun 03:00) and `DreamyCafe Restore Drill` (every 13 weeks, Mon 09:00) joined the backup and lock-on-logon tasks, each returning `LastTaskResult = 0`. **The restore drill actually passed** — it restored the newest dump into `restore_test`, ran its sanity query and dropped the test database, which is the difference between having backups and having *restorable* backups. Its `Order` count was 0, correct for a seeded shop with no sales; the script asserts the result **is an integer** rather than non-zero, so the drill still proves something on fresh data. **Maintenance turned out to genuinely earn its elevation** rather than having inherited it: `Get-StorageReliabilityCounter` returned `wear=0%` / `health=Healthy` for the `KXG6AZNV256G TOSHIBA` NVMe, and that call is precisely what `RunLevel Highest` buys — unelevated it degrades silently to "counters unavailable", which reads like a healthy result if you are not looking closely.
- **BitLocker's prerequisites are now measured facts instead of an open question, and it is still deliberately off.** **TPM 2.0 present, ready, enabled and owned** (Infineon, spec 2.0); **Secure Boot enabled**; `C:` **`FullyDecrypted`, ProtectionStatus `Off`**. TPM-only BitLocker would therefore unlock unattended with no pre-boot PIN — the only shape compatible with the 04:00 unattended update reboot. Not enabled yet, because the decision has a real failure mode rather than a checkbox: **the recovery key has to be retrievable at the moment this machine is the thing that is broken** — so not on `C:`, and not solely in the Google account this box signs into. That is a human step, recorded in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md).

### Fixed
- **The physical power button was set to "Shut down" — one brush against a mini PC on a counter would have killed the till, and the box would not have come back on its own.** Now **"Do nothing"** on AC and DC. The subtle part is the interaction with the BIOS work: **"After Power Loss → Power On" only fires after an *unclean* loss**, so a clean button-press shutdown is exactly the case that rule does not cover — the machine would sit dark until someone physically returned, with no tills, no 21:30 backup and no Tailscale. A 4-second hold still force-powers-off at firmware level, so hang recovery is unaffected. The setting is **hidden by Windows** by default and had to be unhidden (`Attributes = 2`) before it could even be read, which is why it survived earlier passes unnoticed. Also set the Start-menu power button from **Sleep** → **Shut down** (it contradicted every sleep timeout already disabled on this box) and hid Sleep/Hibernate from the Start power flyout.
