- **Disabled hibernation and Fast Startup (`powercfg /h off`) — this is load-bearing for the lock-on-logon guard, not tidying.** With Fast Startup enabled, "shut down" hibernates the kernel session and the next power-on **resumes** it, and `LastBootUpTime` can still report the *previous* full boot. The **uptime guard** added to [lock-on-logon.ps1](scripts/lock-on-logon.ps1) would then see a large uptime, skip, and **leave the autologon desktop unlocked** — the precise failure it exists to prevent, reintroduced through an unrelated-looking Windows default. Making the physical shutdown path easier to reach in the same pass would have made it more likely. Fixing the OS setting was chosen over making the guard cleverer, because `powercfg /a` now reports Fast Startup **unavailable** and that is a fact you can check in one command rather than reason about. Recorded as an explicit ⚠ dependency in the script header, since re-enabling hibernation would break it silently. Also freed 6.3 GB. Note `HiberbootEnabled` still reads `1` in the registry and is inert — `powercfg /a` is the authoritative check.
- **`db-maintenance.ps1` wrote no log file, so the hidden monthly run threw away the one number it exists to collect.** SMART wear is only meaningful as a **trend**, and the scheduled run is hidden, monthly, and sends its output to a discarded stdout — a task returning `0` with no record was indistinguishable from a task that did nothing. It now writes `logs/db-maintenance.log` using the same convention as [restore-drill.ps1](scripts/restore-drill.ps1) (console + in-memory ping body + best-effort file). Verified by re-running the **hidden scheduled** task and reading the wear figure back out of the log.
- **Defender exclusions added for PostgreSQL, scoped deliberately narrowly:** `C:\PostgreSQL\data` and the `postgres.exe` process — **not** `C:\PostgreSQL\bin`, because excluding a directory full of executables mainly creates somewhere for malware to sit unscanned. Real-time protection remains on everywhere else. Also confirmed after three reboots that the Wi-Fi adapter's `SelectiveSuspend` is **Disabled** and USB selective suspend is `0x0` on both AC and DC — that one was set earlier and needed confirming rather than assuming, since the USB dongle is the box's only link to Tailscale.

### Added
- **The mini PC now has working nightly backups — and registering them uncovered that this box was quietly mixing its dumps in with the old shop's.** New [scripts/register-backup-task.ps1](scripts/register-backup-task.ps1) registers `DreamyCafe DB Backup` (daily 21:30, `-StartWhenAvailable`), replacing the hand-edited `Register-ScheduledTask` snippet in [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) §3 that still contained a literal `C:\path\to\DreamyCafe` for the reader to substitute — it was the only one of the four scheduled tasks without a registration script. **The task is deliberately registered as the interactive user at normal privilege, and that is a correctness requirement rather than a shortcut:** `G:\My Drive` is mounted by Google Drive for Desktop **inside the logged-on user session**, so a task registered "run whether user is logged on or not" (S4U) — or elevated with a different token, which cannot always see user-session drive mounts — would produce a *successful local dump with no off-site copy*, the same silent half-failure the autologon work exists to prevent. A pleasant consequence is that it needs no Administrator shell at all, so the backup gap closed immediately instead of waiting for an elevated pass. **Proven through the scheduler rather than by running the script by hand**, since those exercise different tokens: `Start-ScheduledTask` → `LastTaskResult = 0`, a new dump present in the cloud folder, `NextRunTime` 21:30.
- **Backups now mirror to a per-machine Google Drive subfolder, fixing a restore-the-wrong-shop trap that the first manual backup walked straight into.** `$GDriveSubfolder` in [backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1) is now `DreamyCafe-Backups\$env:COMPUTERNAME`. Listing the Drive folder before registering anything showed **the old shop PC is still running and still backing up to the same Google account** — an unbroken nightly series through `dreamycafe_2026-07-29_213002.dump`, timestamped two hours earlier that evening, which cannot have come from this machine because it had no backup task registered yet. So this box's freshly **seeded demo data** (96 KB) was landing beside the old shop's **real production dumps** (108 KB), under `dreamycafe_<stamp>.dump` names that differ only by timestamp, in a folder **both machines prune**. Nothing in a filename or a file size tells you whose database you are holding, and the moment that matters is the one where someone grabs "the newest dump" under pressure. The stray dump this discovery produced was removed from the shared root; the old machine's history was left untouched. Documented in [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md), [RUNBOOK.md](RUNBOOK.md) and [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) — the migrate doc now says to take dumps from the **old** machine's subfolder, which is exactly the step this change makes possible to get wrong.

### Fixed
- **Four docs still instructed the reader to put the database password into a committed script — the precise mechanism by which this project leaked a password into a public repo once already.** [backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1) was fixed some time ago to read `PGPASSWORD` or parse `DATABASE_URL` out of the gitignored `.env`, but §9 of [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) still stated the password was "**hardcoded** in `scripts/backup-db.ps1`", its troubleshooting table told you to update it in "**both**" places, and [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) §4 carried the same instruction as a checklist item. Stale documentation of this kind is worse than none: it reads as authoritative and it is followed. All corrected to name `.env` as the single source, with an explicit warning **not** to "simplify" it back. Also corrected in the same sweep: [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) and [production-setup.md](docs/production-setup.md) both claimed the `scripts\*.ps1` files embed absolute project paths needing hand-editing after a move. They do not — every one derives the project root from `$PSScriptRoot`. What *does* store a path is the **registration**: scheduled tasks and NSSM services record the location they were registered with, so moving the folder means re-running the `register-*.ps1` scripts, which is a different and much smaller action than editing scripts.
- **The healthchecks are now separated from the old PC — and verifying it needed more than exit codes, because a wrong ping URL cannot fail a task.** Three new healthchecks.io checks were created for `DREAMY-CAFE` and all three `.env` keys replaced in one edit; a partial swap would have been worse than none, since [db-maintenance.ps1](scripts/db-maintenance.ps1) falls back to `BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL` when `MAINTENANCE_HEALTHCHECK_URL` is unset and would have silently produced a hybrid. **The verification is the interesting part.** `Send-Ping` swallows a failed ping into a `Write-Warning`, which a hidden scheduled task discards — so `LastTaskResult = 0` proves nothing about whether a ping ever landed. All three scripts were therefore run **directly**, with the warning stream visible and none appearing. That still leaves a trap: an **empty** value produces no warning either, because `Send-Ping` returns early and pings nothing at all, so "no warning" is ambiguous between success and never-ran. Each URL was then POSTed through the scripts' **own `Get-EnvValue` reader** — the same code path, not a reimplementation — returning **HTTP 200 `OK`**, with a control ping to an invalid UUID returning **400** to establish that a bad URL really would have been caught. **The old PC's checks were deliberately not deleted**, and that is the load-bearing decision: its `.env` still holds those URLs, so deleting the checks would turn its pings into 404s absorbed by the same discarded warning, leaving a live production machine with no monitoring *and no announcement of that fact* — strictly worse than the over-optimistic check it replaced. They get deleted after that machine stops and its checks have been watched going down.
- **~~`BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL` is set on this machine but is almost certainly the old PC's check, which breaks the dead-man's switch in the direction nobody notices.~~** *(Resolved same day — see the entry above.)* The `.env` was carried over from the old machine and that key came with it. Two machines pinging one healthchecks.io check means the check reports **up** whenever *either* succeeds — so this box's backups could fail every night for a month behind a green check kept alive by the old PC. The failure mode of a monitoring system is worth stating plainly: it does not go quiet, it goes *reassuring*. Flagged 🔴 in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) with the fix (a second check named for this machine) and the verification step, and the earlier "verified-good, needs no action" note about the three URLs being distinct was amended — they are distinct **from each other**, which was the only thing that check ever established. Requires a human: creating the check and pasting a new URL into `.env`.

### Added
- **Autologon-plus-immediate-lock is in place, closing the off-site backup gap the password change opened — and the "obvious" guard for it turned out to be exactly backwards.** Setting a password on the `User` account ended the box's accidental auto sign-in, and **Google Drive for Desktop runs in a user session rather than as a service**, so after the ~04:00 forced update reboot nobody would be signed in, `G:\My Drive` would never mount, and the off-site mirror in [backup-db.ps1:206](scripts/backup-db.ps1#L206) would stop. The nasty part is the shape of that failure: the **local** backup keeps succeeding, so nothing looks wrong. Enabled with **Sysinternals Autologon** (downloaded and **Authenticode-verified as Microsoft** before running, same discipline as the PostgreSQL installer) rather than a hand-edited `Winlogon` key, because the tool stores the credential **LSA-encrypted** while the registry method every guide shows writes it as **plaintext under `HKLM`**. Verified after the fact: `AutoAdminLogon = 1`, `DefaultUserName`/`DefaultDomainName` set, and **no `DefaultPassword` value present** — the check that distinguishes the two methods. The password was never handled by the agent; a human typed it into the tool. New [scripts/lock-on-logon.ps1](scripts/lock-on-logon.ps1) and [scripts/register-lock-on-logon-task.ps1](scripts/register-lock-on-logon-task.ps1) follow the repo's existing `register-*-task.ps1` convention so the whole arrangement is repeatable if the box is rebuilt, and the task was registered **before** autologon was enabled so the first unattended boot was never left on an unlocked desktop. **Proven end-to-end by rebooting:** `GoogleDriveFS` started at **boot + 27 s** — not at the time of the human's RDP connection minutes later, which is what makes it evidence of an unattended session rather than a normal login — the lock task ran at boot + 15 s with result `0`, `G:\My Drive` was mounted, and `postgresql-x64-18` and `Tailscale` both returned Automatic/Running.

### Fixed
- **The lock task's session guard was wrong in exactly the configuration it was written for, and it locked a live remote session to prove it.** The guard skipped unless `$env:SESSIONNAME -eq 'Console'`, the intent being "don't lock a human who just connected over RDP". It tested clean *before* autologon existed — an RDP logon then created a fresh session reporting `RDP-Tcp#0`, so the check appeared to work, and that early pass is precisely what made it look verified. **With autologon the console session already exists, and an RDP connection takes it over** (Windows 11 Pro permits one interactive session per user — the same session-follows-the-user behaviour documented earlier for RDP), so processes report `SESSIONNAME = 'Console'` and the guard passes and locks the connected human. Replaced with an **uptime** guard: lock only if the machine booted within the last 5 minutes, since the only logon this task should ever act on is the one Windows performs by itself at boot, and any logon minutes later is a person. Also added a `-DryRun` switch so the guard can be exercised without locking anyone, and **the script now writes to `logs/lock-on-logon.log`** (already gitignored) — necessary because **Task Scheduler history and the 4800 "workstation locked" audit are both off by default on this box**, meaning a successful unattended lock and a skipped one produce an identical exit code and no other trace. Re-verified by running the corrected script for real in the live RDP session: it logged `skipped - booted 7.2 min ago` and locked nothing. No re-registration needed — the task invokes the script by path. Ops tooling only; no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched.

### Added
- **The mini PC now survives a power cut unattended, and it was proven by cutting mains power to a running machine rather than by reading a BIOS setting back.** The BIOS change is **"After Power Loss" → Power On** (plus **S5 Maximum Power Savings → Disabled**, which otherwise cuts standby power far enough to defeat auto-power-on — a setting that silently negates the first one and would only be discovered after a real outage). This is the deliberate complement to the earlier decision **not** to buy a UPS: the shop closes during an outage so riding *through* one is pointless, but coming back *after* one is not optional. Without it a 3am blip leaves the box dark until someone physically presses the button — no tills, no 21:30 backup, no Tailscale, and no way to know remotely, because the symptom of "unreachable" is identical to a dozen benign causes. **The evidence, since "I changed the setting" is not the same claim as "it works":** the Windows System log records `Kernel-Power` **id 41** and `EventLog` **id 6008** ("previous system shutdown was unexpected") at 23:16, immediately followed by a boot at 23:16:29 — an unclean stop and an unattended restart, which is precisely the real-world failure rather than a simulation of it. **PostgreSQL took the dirty hit and recovered, and logged it explicitly:** `database system was not properly shut down; automatic recovery in progress`, then `redo starts`/`redo done` and an end-of-recovery checkpoint, with no errors. The data was then **counted rather than assumed intact** — 3 users, 4 categories, 15 products, 6 tables, 2 printer configs, 49 migrations applied and **0 failed or incomplete**, `pg_is_in_recovery() = false`. Both `postgresql-x64-18` and `Tailscale` returned **Automatic/Running** by themselves. Two things worth keeping: **`data_checksums` is `on`** (the PostgreSQL 18 default), so page-level corruption from a future hard cut is *detected* rather than silently served — which materially changes the risk calculus of running without a UPS, alongside the already-confirmed write-cache buffer flushing; and this test belongs on a **fresh, seeded** database, because the first hard power cut of a POS host's life should not happen during trading. **Deliberately not done: the BIOS boot lockdown.** Disabling USB/PXE boot only means anything *together with* a BIOS Administrator password — otherwise anyone can press F10 and re-enable it, so the lockdown alone is decorative. The asset in question is not really the customer data but **`.env`, which holds a live Square *production* access token**. Against that: an HP BIOS password is **not recoverable**, it blocks a legitimate recovery-USB boot at exactly the worst moment, and HP business boards generally have a password-clear header — so it deters an opportunist with a USB stick, not anyone with a screwdriver. Recorded in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) as a decision keyed to where the box physically sits, with the note that the control actually covering both theft and tampering is BitLocker (TPM-only — **never** a pre-boot PIN, which would hang the unattended 4am update reboot). Machine/firmware configuration only — no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched.

### Added
- **The app is now installed, migrated, seeded, built and smoke-tested on the mini PC — and the rebrand was verified in the compiled output rather than trusted.** Run deliberately in an **unelevated** shell: creating `node_modules` and `.next` as Administrator on a box that runs unelevated day-to-day is a reliable source of later permission oddities. `npm install` pulled 229 packages in 45 s, `prisma migrate deploy` applied every migration, `npm run db:seed` produced **1 admin, 1 manager, 1 staff, 4 categories, 15 products, 6 tables, 2 printer configs**, the build completed clean, and `npm test` passed **59/59**. **Three things are worth recording because each looked like a non-event and wasn't.** **(1) npm 11.16 silently blocked four package install scripts** — `@prisma/client`, `@prisma/engines`, `prisma` and `sharp` — behind its new `allow-scripts` gate, emitting only a warning. On most projects that means a missing Prisma client and a broken image pipeline, discovered much later. It is harmless *here for a checked reason rather than by luck*: `npm run build` invokes `prisma generate` explicitly, and sharp 0.34 ships its native binary as a prebuilt optional dependency — confirmed by actually loading it rather than reasoning about it (`require('sharp')` → 0.34.5). The durable warning: don't assume a future `npm ci` on this box generated the Prisma client. **(2) Creating the database ran into a credential problem worth solving properly.** `createdb -U postgres` **prompts interactively**, which a non-interactive agent shell cannot answer, and this project's standing rule forbids moving secrets through chat. Rather than ask for the password, `DATABASE_URL` was parsed out of `.env` in-process and the password handed to `psql` through **`PGPASSWORD` in the child environment only** — never echoed, never logged, never written to a tracked file. This is the pattern to reuse for any future DB bootstrap here. **(3) The smoke-test expectation recorded in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) was wrong and has been corrected**: it asserted `http://localhost:3000/pos` must return **200**, but `/pos` is auth-gated, so an unauthenticated request correctly returns **307** to `/login` — a 200 there would be the bug. Verified codes are `/pos` 307, `/` 307 (no `x-dreamy-zone` header ⇒ LAN zone ⇒ redirect to `/pos`, i.e. the zone split behaving), and `/login`, `/privacy`, `/loyalty`, `/order` all 200. **The rebrand was then checked against the artifact, not the source:** 576 `Dreamy Cafe` strings in `.next`, and the 43 surviving `DreamyCafe` strings all live in `.js` chunks (the `?? 'DreamyCafe'` fallback literals) and `.map` source maps — **zero in any rendered HTML** — with `/manifest.json` and `/offline.html` fetched from the running server to confirm both serve the real name. Left alone on purpose: Prisma is 5.22.0 with 7.9.1 available, and a major upgrade is not something to attempt between now and opening. **One deliberate landmine:** `RECEIPT_PHONE` is set to the literal `CHANGE_ME` so the build could proceed, and **it prints on the tax invoice**. An obviously-wrong token was chosen over a plausible-looking fake number precisely because a well-formed fake would print, look right, and never be noticed. It is runtime-read ([printer.js:29](src/lib/printer.js#L29)), so replacing it needs no rebuild — but it must be a real number before the first sale.

### Changed
- **The rebrand to "Dreamy Cafe" was advertised as a two-env-var change; it isn't, and the gap includes a slip handed to a customer.** [rebrand-checklist.md](docs/rebrand-checklist.md) §1 calls the env vars "the fast path" and lists the five files `BRAND_NAME` covers — accurate as far as it goes, but grepping `src/` for the literal turned up **11 more hardcoded `DreamyCafe` strings that never read `BRAND_NAME` at all**, so following the checklist to completion still ships the placeholder name. The worst is [OrderDetailModal.js:34](src/components/admin/shared/OrderDetailModal.js#L34): a `<h2>DreamyCafe</h2>` written into the browser-print window, which is the **receipt reprint** — a physical slip given to a customer, printing a name the business doesn't trade under. [onlineCheckout.js:161](src/lib/onlineCheckout.js#L161) is the same class of problem one step removed: `paymentNote` reads `DreamyCafe order #N` and lands in Square's record of the payment. Also customer-visible: the loyalty page header ([loyalty/page.js:78](src/app/loyalty/page.js#L78)), the public root's tab title ([page.js:5](src/app/page.js#L5)), and the printed-report header ([PrintReport.js:16](src/components/shared/PrintReport.js#L16)); staff- and accountant-facing are the nav bar, login heading, PWA install hint, the Square reconcile card's body copy, the accounting CSV export header, and the `'DreamyCafe counter'` Square device label. **All five customer-facing sites are now fixed by routing them through `BRAND_NAME`** — importing `@/lib/brand` rather than hardcoding a second literal, which is the difference between fixing this once and re-doing it at the next rename. `onlineCheckout.js` is the deliberate exception: it uses **`DOCKET_BRAND_NAME`** instead, matching the four existing money-artifact call sites ([printer.js:27](src/lib/printer.js#L27), [zReport.js:20](src/lib/zReport.js#L20)), because the note is attached to a payment record rather than shown in the storefront — and as a runtime env read it also survives a rebrand without a rebuild, unlike `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`. **Checked before touching the payment path:** nothing anywhere reads `paymentNote` back — it is write-only to Square, so no reconciliation logic depends on its shape. The trap avoided is that `order.note` is a **completely different field** carrying online pickup name/phone, parsed by `parsePickupName`/`parsePickupNote` in [printer.js:161](src/lib/printer.js#L161), [fulfillment.js:51](src/lib/fulfillment.js#L51) and [online/page.js:117](src/app/online/page.js#L117) — a find-and-replace across "note" would have broken pickup names on kitchen dockets. Also fixed: the two static files the checklist correctly flags ([public/manifest.json](public/manifest.json) `name`/`short_name`/`description`, [public/offline.html](public/offline.html) title), which matter more than their size suggests because they **cannot** read env vars and the manifest name is baked into the PWA install — a wrong value persists on the iPad home screen until the app is reinstalled. **The six staff-facing sites followed in the same pass** — nav bar, login heading, PWA install hint, Square reconcile card copy, the accounting CSV export header (`DOCKET_BRAND_NAME`, since it is an accountant's artifact and [buildSummaryCsv](src/lib/accounting.js#L228) is only ever called server-side from [api/reports/export](src/app/api/reports/export/route.js#L33), so a `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` read wasn't needed), and the Square device-label default. The nav bar needed one small addition: it hardcoded **`DC`** as the narrow-screen abbreviation, a brand mark that a name-only rebrand silently leaves behind, so `BRAND_INITIALS` now derives it from `BRAND_NAME` (word initials, falling back to the first two characters for a single-word name) — "Dreamy Cafe" → `IC`. **One site is deliberately left wrong:** [square-pair/route.js:12](src/app/api/terminal/square-pair/route.js#L12) still reads `'DreamyCafe counter'` because `src/app/api/{orders,terminal,webhooks}/**` is in this project's never-touch set, and a cosmetic device label is nowhere near worth the exception. It is only the *default* — the pairing UI can pass any name, and the label is renameable in the Square dashboard — but note that [square.js:65](src/lib/square.js#L65) was updated regardless and the route overrides it, so the literal is what actually ships until it is renamed at pairing time. **A full sweep confirms the app itself is now clean:** every remaining `DreamyCafe` under `src/`, `public/` and the service worker is either a `?? 'DreamyCafe'` fallback that by design never fires once the env vars are set, or a code comment. `public/icon.svg` turned out to be a wordless coffee-cup mark, so there is no logo work hiding behind this. **What the sweep did surface is `marketing/`** — a separate Cloudflare Pages deploy, outside the POS build and therefore outside this change, but the most public asset the shop has: six visible DreamyCafe strings in `index.html` plus `DREAMYCAFE_ORDER_ORIGIN` still pointing at `https://dreamy-cafe.com`. Deliberately deferred rather than forgotten — rebranding that copy before the real domain is decided means doing it twice — and recorded as its own open item in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md). No money, auth, or zone logic touched; the two payment-path edits are display strings with no reader.
- **The three absent `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` keys now exist in `.env` as explicit `CHANGE_ME` placeholders rather than as absent keys that silently work.** This is the whole point: all three have fallbacks ([brand.js:1-2](src/lib/brand.js#L1-L2), [privacy/page.js:19](src/app/privacy/page.js#L19)), so an unset key produces a clean build, a working app, and the wrong brand — there is no error to notice. `NEXT_PUBLIC_BUSINESS_NAME` was set to `Dreamy Cafe` because it is not a judgement call (`DOCKET_BRAND_NAME` in the same file already says so); `NEXT_PUBLIC_BUSINESS_TAGLINE` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_PRIVACY_EMAIL` are left as literal `CHANGE_ME` for a human. The privacy email is the one that actually bites: its fallback is `privacy@dreamy-cafe.com`, **a domain the shop does not own**, so publishing the policy as-is lists a contact address that goes nowhere — a legal-contact failure, not a cosmetic one. `RECEIPT_PHONE` was left empty rather than given a placeholder, since a `CHANGE_ME` on a tax invoice is worse than a blank. Also corrected in the handoff: the 🔴 `DATABASE_URL` retired-password item was still unticked despite having been rotated and **verified by rejection** earlier the same day — a stale red flag on a resolved item trains the next reader to skim past red flags. ⚠ Reminder recorded inline in `.env`: `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` is **inlined at `npm run build`**, not read at runtime, so these must be right *before* the build or it must be redone. No app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched.

### Added
- **The mini PC's prerequisite stack is installed and the Windows Update policy is live — with the DB password rotation *proved* rather than assumed.** Node.js **24.18.0 LTS** at the default path (confirming `C:\Program Files\nodejs\npm.cmd` exists, which is the one path [install-windows-services.ps1:16](scripts/install-windows-services.ps1#L16) hardcodes), **PostgreSQL 18.4-2** into `C:\PostgreSQL` (Authenticode-verified as EnterpriseDB *before* running the downloaded installer), **cloudflared 2026.7.3**, and **Google Drive for Desktop 128.0.0.0**. The PostgreSQL installer was launched **interactively on purpose** — winget's silent path would have set a superuser password this project must never let an agent choose, so the wizard was pre-seeded with `--prefix`/`--datadir`/`--serverport` and the password left entirely to a human. **The rotation was then verified by attempting to authenticate with the retired password and confirming the server rejects it** — the failure this build exists to prevent is typing the old password at the installer out of habit, which looks identical to success and silently re-adopts a credential that was public on GitHub. Worth recording that Postgres exposure is gated *twice*: `listen_addresses = '*'` looks alarming alone, but `pg_hba.conf` permits only `127.0.0.1/32` and `::1/128` **and** no inbound firewall rule for 5432 exists, so the bind address is moot. **cloudflared was checked for the thing that actually matters** — that the MSI registered **no service** and left **no process running**. A second machine running the same named tunnel splits customer traffic across two databases silently, so "installed but definitively not running" is the required state at home, not merely the observed one. **Windows Update policy applied** via [configure-windows-updates.ps1](scripts/configure-windows-updates.ps1): active hours 06:00–18:00, forced restart 04:00 with a 15-minute countdown (after the 21:30 backup and 03:00 maintenance), a 4-day quality-update settle so a bad Patch Tuesday build is usually pulled first, and the feature version **pinned** to the detected 25H2 (`TargetReleaseVersion = 1`, `TargetReleaseVersionInfo = 25H2`) so a 15–30 minute semi-annual upgrade can never install itself mid-trade. [register-feature-update-reminder-task.ps1](scripts/register-feature-update-reminder-task.ps1) registered the twice-yearly reminder; note its **first** fire is 2026-08-02 rather than 26 weeks out, because a `-Weekly -WeeksInterval 26` trigger starts at the next matching Sunday and only then repeats — harmless for a reminder, but it should not be mistaken for a misconfiguration. Two process notes for anyone repeating this: the script's Administrator guard **correctly refused to half-apply** when VS Code had been relaunched unelevated between steps, and a child `powershell.exe -File …` does **not** reliably inherit elevation in this harness, so the scripts must be invoked in-process with `& .\scripts\…`. Also confirmed rather than assumed: **write-cache buffer flushing is untouched** — no `CacheIsPowerProtected` value exists anywhere under `Enum\…\Device Parameters\Disk`, so the deliberate UPS stand-in is genuinely in place. **Still human-only and outstanding:** Google Drive is installed but **not signed in** (no `GoogleDriveFS`, no `G:`), and folder-sync for Desktop/Documents must be left **off** or it uploads `.env`. Ops/deployment only — no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched.

### Fixed
- **The mini PC was configured to go to sleep after 15 minutes idle — on a machine whose entire job is to be awake 24/7.** Found while configuring power settings rather than by a failure, which is the only good way to find it: `powercfg /query SCHEME_CURRENT SUB_SLEEP` reported `STANDBYIDLE = 0x384` — 900 seconds — the Windows 11 Balanced default that nobody changes because desktops are normally attended. On this box the consequences are the whole system: **the POS stops serving the tills, RDP and Tailscale go unreachable, the 21:30 backup never runs, and the 03:00 maintenance job never runs** — and because the machine is headless with no one watching, the failure presents as "the tills stopped working" rather than anything diagnostic. Worse, it would have looked *fine* during setup, since an attended box being actively worked on never idles long enough to trip it. Sleep, hibernate and disk-spindown are now all `0` (never) on **both AC and DC** profiles. **The genuinely dangerous one was USB selective suspend, which was enabled.** This machine's only live network link is a **USB WiFi dongle** (`Realtek RTL8188FTV`, the built-in `Ethernet 2` is unplugged until the shop) — so USB power management can suspend the network adapter itself, dropping the box off the tailnet and taking remote access with it. That is precisely the failure you cannot recover from remotely, on a machine that is meant to be administered remotely. Disabled at the power-plan level (AC and DC) and at the device level via `PnPCapabilities = 24` on both physical adapters, which turns off *"Allow the computer to turn off this device to save power"* — **`Ethernet 2` had it unset entirely** (power management fully permitted) and the WiFi dongle was at `16`, which covers wake behaviour but not the power-down. The adapters were deliberately **not** restarted: bouncing the WiFi mid-session would have dropped the live RDP connection, and the values apply at next boot, which is coming anyway. **Also fixed: the clock, flagged as unsynchronized in the 2026-07-28 audit.** `w32time` was on `Manual` with `Source: Local CMOS Clock`, `Stratum 0`, `Leap Indicator 3 (not synchronized)` and `Last Successful Sync Time: unspecified` — meaning the timezone being correct proved nothing, and every report, receipt timestamp and backup filename would have keyed off a free-running CMOS clock. Set to `Automatic`, peered to `time.windows.com`/`time.nist.gov`, now `Leap Indicator 0`, `Stratum 5`, offset ~1 ms measured by `w32tm /stripchart`. Worth recording because the **first `w32tm /resync` failed** with "no time data available" and only succeeded after a service restart — a single resync attempt returning an error is not evidence the clock can't sync. The same "verify, don't assume" applies to the last item: `net user User /passwordreq:yes` **silently no-opped on the first run** (no output, and `Password required` still read `No`); a second run took, and `UserFlags` now reads `0x10201` with `PASSWD_NOTREQD` clear, closing the leftover exemption from the blank-password era. Machine configuration only — no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched. **Still open on this box:** the WiFi adapter's own `SelectiveSuspend` property continues to report `Enabled` (the driver exposes no registry keyword for it), so it needs `Set-NetAdapterPowerManagement` or a post-reboot recheck; the plan-level and `PnPCapabilities` controls are the dominant ones and are now off. `Fast Startup` remains flagged on in the registry but is **inert** — `hiberfil.sys` does not exist, so hibernation is already unavailable, and the 4am update reboot is a restart, which never uses Fast Startup regardless.

### Changed
- **Remote access to the mini PC is now actually working, not just configured — the missing step was a reboot, and the settings that looked correct throughout were the reason it took a while.** [RUNBOOK.md](RUNBOOK.md) §7A already flagged the two one-time toggles that keep remote access alive (Tailscale key expiry, password expiry), but nothing recorded how to *get there* on a fresh box, and the failure mode is genuinely misleading. Remote Desktop needed **two** things. First, the `User` account had **no password at all**, and Windows refuses RDP for blank-password accounts regardless of every other setting (`LimitBlankPasswordUse` governs this and is on by default) — so RDP could never have worked no matter how many toggles were flipped. Second, and the non-obvious half: after enabling RDP on a running machine **nothing listened on port 3389 until a full reboot**. `Restart-Service TermService -Force` does *not* substitute for it — it fails with `StopServiceFailed`, and notably `CanStop` reports `True`, so "it's a protected service" is **not** the explanation. Throughout that period every value a check would normally trust read correct: `fDenyTSConnections = 0`, NLA on, all three `Remote Desktop` firewall rules enabled, `TermService` `Running` — while connections were still refused. **The only check that settles it is a real connection attempt**: `Test-NetConnection 127.0.0.1 -Port 3389` must return `TcpTestSucceeded = True`. Enumerating services or reading the registry proves nothing here, and time was lost trusting those readings; that lesson is recorded in the handoff rather than left to be re-learned on the next build. Post-reboot state verified end-to-end: `TcpTestSucceeded = True`, `0.0.0.0:3389 LISTENING` on both IPv4 and IPv6, `TermService`/`SessionEnv`/`UmRdpService` all `Running`, and Tailscale back up on its own as an `Automatic` service at `100.105.157.110` — the reboot-survival property the headless box depends on, now demonstrated rather than assumed. The account password is also set to **never expire** (`PasswordExpires` blank), closing the 42-day `MaxPasswordAge` inheritance trap RUNBOOK §7A warns about, where a lapsed password kills RDP and forces a physical trip to the shop. **The machine was renamed** from the factory `DESKTOP-XXXXXXX` to **`DREAMY-CAFE`** (the Tailscale node is `dreamy-cafe`), so RDP logins are `DREAMY-CAFE\User` — [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md) updated, and the rename does **not** affect anything in the repo, since no script references the hostname and the scripts all derive their root from `$PSScriptRoot`. **Off-LAN access is now proven, and the way it was proven is the point.** The meaningful test is not "can I reach it over Tailscale" but "does it work when I am genuinely somewhere else", and **two attempts passed the naive check while failing the real one**: RDP connected over the tailnet address and `tailscale status` reported `direct`, but the endpoint in use was `192.168.0.2:41641` — a **private** address on the mini PC's own `192.168.0.0/24` subnet. The laptop still held a home-WiFi association alongside the phone hotspot, Tailscale correctly preferred the fast local route, and the traffic never left the building. The first retest made it worse: turning the laptop's WiFi off killed the hotspot *as well*, because the hotspot **was** a WiFi network — the laptop dropped off the tailnet entirely. USB-tethering the phone first, *then* disabling WiFi, is the combination that produces an unambiguous test, since no WiFi interface remains for a local path to sneak through. The pass: `CurAddr = 101.113.179.40:1705`, a public carrier address, **`direct` rather than DERP-relayed**, 41 ms against 11 ms on the LAN, with `Test-Connection 192.168.0.2` returning no reply to confirm the laptop had genuinely left the local segment. A *direct* path through mobile carrier-grade NAT is the better of the two acceptable outcomes and matches what `netcheck` predicted for this box (`UDP: true`, `MappingVariesByDestIP: false`, `PortMapping: UPnP`) — relay via Sydney DERP would have worked too, just slower. The diagnostic worth keeping: **check that `CurAddr` is a public IP**; "it connected over Tailscale" is not evidence of anything, and an ARP entry or a successful `Test-Connection` to the peer's private address is proof that it is not. This result also does **not** carry over to the shop — the box moves to wired ethernet behind a different router, so the NAT behaviour changes and it needs re-testing there. **Tailscale key expiry is now disabled for `dreamy-cafe`**, which was the other half of durable remote access: the device key was due to lapse on 2027-01-25, ~180 days out, at which point a headless box drops off the tailnet and needs `tailscale up` — an *interactive browser login* — to come back, i.e. a trip to the shop with a keyboard, discovered at the exact moment remote access was needed. Verified on the node rather than trusting the console badge: `tailscale status --json` no longer reports a `Self.KeyExpiry` field at all. The laptop's key deliberately still expires (179 days) — it is attended and can simply re-authenticate, and disabling expiry weakens a genuine security control, so it is spent only on the machine that physically cannot log itself back in. **One item still deliberately open.** Setting a password **ends the box's automatic sign-in** — there was no autologon, it simply booted straight in because the account was blank. NSSM services are unaffected, but **Google Drive for Desktop runs in a user session**, so after the ~4am forced update reboot documented in the Windows Update entry below, nobody is signed in, `G:\My Drive` never mounts, and off-site backup mirroring stops. Nothing is broken today (Drive isn't installed yet), and the condition is *already alarmed* — [backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1) checks `GoogleDriveFS` is running and soft-fails the healthcheck — but it must be solved with autologon-plus-immediate-lock before off-site backups can be relied on. Also noted for the elevated pass: `PasswordRequired` is still `False` (the `PASSWD_NOTREQD` flag survives from the blank-password era), which doesn't affect RDP now that a password exists but leaves the account exempt from password policy — `net user User /passwordreq:yes`. Machine configuration and docs only — no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched, and nothing installed yet.

### Security
- **The `.env` copied onto the new mini PC still pointed `DATABASE_URL` at the retired Postgres password — the exact failure [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) step 4 predicts, caught before PostgreSQL was installed rather than after.** The file is copied *verbatim* from the old machine by design, so the stale credential rides along silently; the doc's warning only helps if someone actually checks. Audited it **without printing any secret value** — the check script reports key names, character counts and boolean comparisons only, and prints values solely for non-secret keys (currency, printer IPs, receipt details, `SQUARE_ENV`). The password field was compared against the retired value and matched. That value was published in the public repo before being scrubbed (see the earlier Security entry), and this build is the planned moment to rotate it, so the resolution is a **new strong password at the installer prompt plus an edit to `DATABASE_URL`** — the failure mode to avoid is "fixing" the mismatch by typing the old password at the installer, which silently re-adopts a credential that was on public GitHub. Recorded as the top open item in [SETUP-HANDOFF.md](SETUP-HANDOFF.md); both halves are human-only, and no secret was written into any tracked file. **Four further gaps found in the same pass.** **(1) The rebrand is half-applied:** `DOCKET_BRAND_NAME`, `RECEIPT_ADDRESS/EMAIL/ABN` are all the real shop, but `NEXT_PUBLIC_BUSINESS_NAME`, `NEXT_PUBLIC_PRIVACY_EMAIL` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_BUSINESS_TAGLINE` are absent, so [brand.js:1](src/lib/brand.js#L1) and [privacy/page.js:19](src/app/privacy/page.js#L19) fall back to `'DreamyCafe'` and `'privacy@dreamy-cafe.com'` — receipts would print the real name while the storefront, browser tab and PWA showed the placeholder. The non-obvious part is **ordering**: `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` is inlined at `npm run build`, so setting these afterwards requires a rebuild — the handoff's command block now flags that inline. **(2)** `RECEIPT_PHONE` is present but **empty**, which would print blank on a tax invoice. **(3) The printer IPs look like untouched placeholders** — `192.168.1.100`/`.101`, while every other signal indicates a `192.168.0.x` LAN (the box sits on `192.168.0.182`, and the shop's front printer is recorded at `192.168.0.12`); left as-is they would simply time out at the shop. **(4)** `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` is a **test** key — inert while Square is active on both channels, but a provider switch at Admin → Payments would silently run card charges in test mode and capture no real money. Also confirmed as a *consequence of the fresh-shop decision* rather than a fault: `SQUARE_DEVICE_ID` is unset and [resolveSquareDeviceId](src/lib/square.js#L35) falls back DB → env, so with the paired Terminal living in the old database (deliberately not restored) in-person charging throws "No payment terminal configured" until it is re-paired at `/admin/terminal`. **Verified good and explicitly recorded so they aren't re-audited:** the file is structurally clean (22 keys, no BOM, LF endings, no duplicates, no malformed lines, confirmed gitignored and invisible to `git status`); Square is genuinely production; `RECEIPT_ABN` is set so receipts print **TAX INVOICE**; `GST_RATE`/`GST_INCLUSIVE` are absent but default to `0.1` and inclusive ([accounting.js:45-46](src/lib/accounting.js#L45-L46)), correct for Australia; and the three healthcheck URLs are **all distinct**, meaning the per-job dead-man's switch added earlier in this changelog is correctly wired on this machine rather than being three copies of one check. `PUBLIC_ZONE_SECRET` is present but **cannot** be verified against the Cloudflare Transform Rule from this box — flagged as a cutover check, since a mismatch exposes the staff zone. Docs only — no code touched, nothing installed, and `.env` itself was not modified.

### Changed
- **`SETUP-HANDOFF.md` now records the mini PC's *audited* state instead of an all-unticked wishlist, and the flagged open decision is resolved: this is a fresh shop, not a data migration.** The handoff was written on the laptop before the box was moved and told the next agent to "confirm with the user exactly which of the boxes below are actually ticked" — so the first useful act on the mini PC was to check rather than assume. Audited on `DESKTOP-XXXXXXX` (HP ProDesk 600 G4 DM, Windows 11 **Pro**, 256 GB NVMe / ~210 GB free — hardware matches the handoff): **Git is installed** (2.55.0.3, alongside GitHub Desktop, VS Code and Chrome) and the **timezone is already `Australia/Sydney`**, but **Node.js, PostgreSQL, cloudflared, Tailscale and Google Drive for Desktop are all absent**, **Remote Desktop is disabled** (`fDenyTSConnections = 1`), no Windows Update policy has been applied and no DreamyCafe scheduled tasks exist. The repo is cloned on branch `Test`, clean and level with `origin/Test`, with **no `.env`, no `node_modules`, no `.next`** — so migrate-doc steps 3–6 had not started. Two findings the checklist wouldn't have surfaced: **(1)** `w32tm /query /status` reports the clock **not synchronized** (Stratum 0, Leap Indicator 3) despite the correct zone — worth a resync before trusting any report date, since reports key off the server clock; **(2)** the box is still on the **USB WiFi dongle** (`192.168.0.182`) with `Ethernet 2` on APIPA `169.254.x`, confirming it is genuinely in home-prep and not yet on the shop LAN, so every shop-only step (tunnel, static IP, NSSM services, reader pairing, Cloudflare) correctly stays untouched. **The username discrepancy was checked rather than worked around.** Every doc writes the project path as `C:\Users\John\Desktop\DreamyCafe`; the actual Windows account is `User`, so it lives at `C:\Users\User\Desktop\DreamyCafe`, and [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) warns that a changed username means editing hardcoded paths in the service and scheduled-task scripts. **It doesn't here** — grepping all 39 files in [scripts/](scripts/) found no hardcoded user path at all; they derive the project root from `$PSScriptRoot`. Only two absolute paths are baked in, and both are install-location constraints rather than edits: `C:\Program Files\nodejs\npm.cmd` ([install-windows-services.ps1:16](scripts/install-windows-services.ps1#L16)) and `C:\PostgreSQL\bin` (the backup/maintenance/restore trio). Recorded in the handoff so the next agent doesn't re-derive it or "helpfully" rewrite working scripts. The **open decision** — fresh shop vs. carrying over an already-trading shop's data — is now answered **fresh**, which means `npm run db:seed` after `prisma migrate deploy` and, importantly, that migrate-doc steps 4 and 7 (locate the `dreamycafe_<stamp>.dump` + matching `uploads_<stamp>.zip` pair, `pg_restore` over the empty DB) **do not apply**; the seeded `admin` = `0000` / `manager` = `1234` PINs are flagged as a human must-change before opening. The stale `git clone` block was replaced with the commands that actually remain, since the clone is already done. Docs only — no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched, and nothing installed yet (the installs need an elevated shell).

### Fixed
- **The dead-man's switch only covered the nightly backup — a monthly or quarterly job that silently *stopped running* would never have been noticed.** Found by auditing the live machine rather than the code: all three scheduled tasks are healthy (`LastTaskResult = 0`, backup ran last night), but `.env` sets **only** `BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL`, so [db-maintenance.ps1](scripts/db-maintenance.ps1) and [restore-drill.ps1](scripts/restore-drill.ps1) were both pinging the backup's healthchecks.io check. Failure alerting worked fine — any job erroring still sends `/fail` and emails you. The **dead-man's half did not**: the backup pings that same check every night, so it can never go silent, and a monthly or quarterly task that quietly stopped (disabled, renamed, or lost in the upcoming mini-PC migration) would have kept a green check indefinitely. That is precisely the silent-failure mode the restore-drill alerting added earlier in this changelog was built to close — it was only half-closed, and the next drill isn't due until **19 Oct**, so the discovery window was months. `restore-drill.ps1` already supported a dedicated `RESTORE_DRILL_HEALTHCHECK_URL`; `db-maintenance.ps1` had **no override at all** and read `BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL` directly, its header even asserting that "sharing one is fine" — true for failures, wrong for silence. It now reads **`MAINTENANCE_HEALTHCHECK_URL` first, falling back to `BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL`**, mirroring the drill exactly so both jobs behave the same way, and the header explains the dead-man's reasoning rather than dismissing it. **Verified all three branches** by extracting the resolver and exercising it out-of-process (so nothing pinged the live check and no URL was printed): dedicated-unset falls back to the backup URL, dedicated-set wins over it, neither-set returns null so alerting disables cleanly instead of erroring. Script re-checked CRLF (201/201), ASCII-only, and parse-clean. Docs corrected where they had over-promised: [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) previously said maintenance "reuses the same healthcheck URL as the backup" as though that were the intended design, and [RUNBOOK.md](RUNBOOK.md) §7 flatly claimed *"everything auto-alerts through healthchecks.io if it fails, so no news is good news"* — both now state the distinction between **failing** (always caught) and **ceasing to run** (only caught with a per-job check), with the recommended periods (~35-day maintenance, ~90-day drill). No behaviour change for anyone who hasn't created the extra checks: the fallback keeps existing alerting working exactly as before. **Follow-up once the checks existed:** the drill's grace period was set to 2 days, which is a false-alarm generator rather than a monitor — the trigger is `-Weekly -WeeksInterval 13`, i.e. **exactly 91 days**, and healthchecks.io alarms at `period + grace`, so 90 + 2 leaves a **one-day margin** that a mini-PC switched off on the scheduled Monday would blow. A false "down" email on a healthy system is worse than no email, because it teaches you to ignore the channel. Raised to a 7-day grace (97-day window, six days of slack, still catches a genuinely dead drill inside a week) and the arithmetic is now recorded in [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) as a table of trigger → real interval → period/grace → margin, so it isn't re-derived by guesswork on the new machine. Same table documents the easily-missed fact that maintenance is **4-weekly (28 days), not calendar-monthly**, so its period must not be set from the word "monthly."

### Changed
- **Script audit: one genuine duplicate removed, one file relocated, and the rest reframed as a cutover checklist — because they aren't redundant yet, they become hazardous on migration day.** Audited all 12 root launchers and the 39 files in [scripts/](scripts/) against the target model (mini-PC runs **services-only**, the iPad is the till, Electron is dev-testing only). The first pass read as a long delete list, but checking the machine's actual state corrected that: `DreamyCafeApp`/`DreamyCafeTunnel` are installed but **Disabled** here — the exact state `disable-windows-services.bat` produces — because the current PC runs in **launcher mode** via `start-pos.bat`. So `start-pos.bat`, `start-pos-hidden.vbs`, `stop-pos.bat`, `stop-pos.ps1` and the enable/disable pair are the *current workflow*, not dead weight, and they stay. **The real finding is a timing hazard.** [desktop/main.js](desktop/main.js) spawns *both* `npm run start` **and** `cloudflared tunnel run dreamycafe` (`spawnServer`/`spawnTunnel`) whenever it doesn't find a server already up — so `start-pos.bat` is not just a kiosk launcher, it brings up the production-named tunnel. Harmless today with one host; on cutover day, one double-click out of habit on the retired PC gives Cloudflare **two replicas of the same named tunnel**, and it load-balances customer traffic across them. That fails *quietly* — the site stays up, nothing alerts, but an unpredictable share of real online orders is written to the wrong machine's database, invisible to the shop and missing from the day's takings. Nothing in the repo warned about this. [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) step 8 now carries that warning explicitly (the old doc said only "one machine may run the tunnel", which reads as a config note rather than a revenue risk), and a new **"Retire the launcher scripts"** subsection under Decommission spells out what to remove from the old PC and why disabling the services isn't sufficient — these scripts bypass services entirely. The same section flags that [allow-lan-pos.ps1](scripts/allow-lan-pos.ps1) and [test-lan-pos.ps1](scripts/test-lan-pos.ps1) are plaintext-era: both assume `http://<ip>:3000`, while [install-caddy-service.ps1](scripts/install-caddy-service.ps1) adds a rule **blocking** inbound 3000 — and Windows Firewall block rules override allow rules, so on a *healthy* TLS setup the test script reports failure and sends you to the allow script, which prints success in green and changes nothing. That matters because [staff-quick-reference.md](docs/staff-quick-reference.md) routes staff down this path for "iPad can't open POS" — the most likely support call once the iPad is the till. Step 9 was corrected for the same drift: it still told you to point tablets at `http://<new-ip>:3000/pos`, and now states the LAN-TLS URL is `https://<new-ip>` with the non-obvious follow-through — the IP is baked into the Caddy service as `POS_LAN_HOST`, so a changed IP means re-running the installer with `-LanHost`, after which Caddy issues a fresh leaf off the **same root CA** and the iPads' one-time root trust still holds (no re-trust). **Actually deleted: `scripts/test-money.js`** — a true duplicate. [test/money.test.js](test/money.test.js) asserts everything it did (float-vs-Decimal, surcharge/discount totals, proportional and 7-way splits, `toCents` half-up, lossless JSON round-trip, null-safety) **plus** `fromCents`/`min`/`max`/`cmp`, and it runs in `npm test` and in CI — while the deleted script was hand-rolled and ran nowhere automatically, i.e. a second copy of the money guarantees that could silently drift from the one being enforced. [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md) now points new money checks at the unit suite instead. **Moved: `fake-printer.js` → [scripts/fake-printer.js](scripts/fake-printer.js)** (dev-only TCP-9100 ESC/POS stub; it was the last loose `.js` at the repo root) and added to the README dev/test table, where it had never been listed. **Also deleted: `start-pos-dev.bat`** — the weakest file of the set. It was two `cmd /k` windows running `npm run dev` plus `cloudflared tunnel run dreamycafe`: the first half is a command you already type, and the second half is the one action that must never happen once the mini-PC is live. Its apparent unique value — testing the customer-facing public zone — turns out not to require a tunnel at all: [isPublicZone()](src/lib/zone.js) only checks that the `x-dreamy-zone` header equals `PUBLIC_ZONE_SECRET`, so setting both reproduces public-zone behaviour locally, and a genuine edge test should use a throwaway quick tunnel rather than the production named one. [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md) records both facts so the file doesn't get recreated, and its "never both" line now states the *consequence* (replica traffic-splitting) rather than just the rule. Verified: `npm test` 59/59 pass, the moved file parses clean, `npm run claude:check` passes, and no dangling references remain in any tracked doc or script.

### Security
- **Removed the production Postgres password from the docs — the half a previous hardening pass missed — and made the new-PC build actually rotate it.** The repo is **public** (`gh repo view` → `visibility: PUBLIC`), and [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) still stated the live DB password in four places: the "database at a glance" table plus three `$env:PGPASSWORD = "…"` restore examples. An earlier pre-publish audit had specifically stripped this value out of `backup-db.ps1`, `db-maintenance.ps1` and `restore-drill.ps1` — its own note records that the first grep missed it "because the value is only 4 chars" — but the **docs were never covered by that sweep**. Not remotely exploitable (Postgres is LAN-only with no inbound path), yet it directly undercuts the LAN-TLS work added earlier in this same changelog, whose entire premise is *"what if someone gains a foothold on the shop network"* — against exactly that threat the password was published on a repo linked from a CV. The glance table now says the password is **not recorded here** and lives in exactly one place, `DATABASE_URL` in the gitignored `.env`, which every ops script already resolves through `Get-DbPassword`; the three examples use `<your-db-password>` with a pointer to `.env`. **Git history deliberately not rewritten** — the owner is rotating the password during the new mini-PC build, which makes the published value worthless and is cheaper and safer than a history rewrite. The follow-through is what makes that plan real: **neither** [migrate-to-new-pc.md](docs/migrate-to-new-pc.md) **nor** [production-setup.md](docs/production-setup.md) previously said a word about the DB password, so the installer prompt would most likely have been answered with the old value out of habit — the migration doc's step even says to "match the old PC," which invites exactly that. Both now carry an explicit step to set a **strong, non-reused** superuser password, with the two facts that make it safe and the one that makes it fail: the password is a *server* setting and is **not** inside the dump (so a new one restores fine), and `.env` is copied verbatim from the old machine, so **`DATABASE_URL` must be edited** or the app silently won't connect. Verified no password literal remains in any tracked doc — the only remaining `1234` matches are documented **seed PINs** (deliberately public, including for the demo login) and one historical CHANGELOG entry describing the original fix, left intact per the same don't-rewrite-history call. Docs only — no code touched.

### Added
- **The portfolio demo no longer deploys a commit that failed the tests.** Visible in the first CI run: a push to `main` produced four checks — the new `Tests` job plus Cloudflare Pages (marketing) and Vercel ×2 (the [portfolio demo](docs/demo-deploy.md) on Neon) — and **Vercel deploys on push regardless of GitHub check status**, it doesn't wait for CI. Since that URL is what a recruiter clicks, a red build could ship straight to it. Fixed by inverting the trigger rather than trying to make Vercel wait: [vercel.json](vercel.json) now sets `git.deploymentEnabled.main = false`, which stops automatic deploys from a push to `main` **while leaving deploy hooks working** — that combination is the mechanism — and a new `deploy-demo` job in [tests.yml](.github/workflows/tests.yml) fires the hook with `needs: test`, so the demo deploys only after a green suite, and only on a push to `main`. Other branches keep normal preview deploys; only the recruiter-facing URL is gated. The job **skips cleanly with a log note when `VERCEL_DEPLOY_HOOK` isn't configured**, so CI can't go red in the window before the secret exists, and uses `curl -f` so a dead hook fails the job instead of silently "succeeding". Deliberately unchanged: this still has nothing to do with the shop PC — production Postgres is local to the mini-PC with no inbound path, so the only CD in this project points at the demo, which is where it belongs. One-time setup (create the Vercel deploy hook, add it as a GitHub Actions secret) plus how to verify the gate actually bites is documented in [demo-deploy.md §2a](docs/demo-deploy.md), including the dashboard "Ignored Build Step" fallback if `deploymentEnabled` doesn't take. Workflow YAML and `vercel.json` both validated (2 jobs, correct `needs`/`if` gating, valid JSON).
- **CI: a single GitHub Actions workflow runs the test suite on every push — and deliberately nothing else.** [scripts/deploy.ps1](scripts/deploy.ps1) already gates deploys on `npm test`, but that runs on the mini-PC against a *working tree*, at the last possible moment (you're RDP'd into the shop box, about to deploy). [.github/workflows/tests.yml](.github/workflows/tests.yml) answers the question the local gate can't: **does the committed state pass?** — catching the classic "works because of something only on my machine," such as a file never `git add`ed. The durability argument is the real one: at 216 commits in 90 days by a single developer, the money/zone/lockout/totals tests are the guardrail, and until now they only ran when someone chose to run them; a red X on a commit is a signal that requires nobody to remember anything and survives the machine being replaced. **Explicitly no CD.** Nothing in CI touches the shop PC: a live POS must not restart itself mid-trade, and the mini-PC has no inbound path by design (Tailscale only) — giving a cloud runner one would undo the security posture. Deploys stay human-timed behind the existing backup → test → build → restart → health-check sequence. Also skipped as pure ceremony for a one-developer/one-box project: branch protection, required reviews, PR-based workflow, staging environments, containers. Implementation notes, each verified rather than assumed: runs on **windows-latest** to match the platform the app actually runs on (the suite is pure logic — no database, no `.env`, no secrets — so Linux works too, **confirmed** by running the exact command under `sh`, where the `test/**/*.test.js` glob passes through unexpanded and Node 22 globs it itself: 59/59 pass; matching the deployment platform simply removes any path/loader difference for a sub-second suite); `npm ci` rather than `npm install` so a drifting lockfile fails loudly in CI instead of quietly on the shop PC (**verified** `npm ci --dry-run` succeeds, so the lockfile is in sync); `concurrency` with `cancel-in-progress` so a rapid follow-up push doesn't pay for a run whose result is already stale; and `permissions: contents: read`. `npm run build` was deliberately **left out** — it runs `prisma generate` then `next build`, which may want a `DATABASE_URL` present even as a dummy, and an unverified step that fails for environmental reasons would train you to ignore the signal. A status badge was added to the [README](README.md), since a red X only helps if it's seen. YAML validated (parses clean, 1 job, 4 steps, no tabs). No app code touched.
- **RUNBOOK §7A now documents which credentials on the mini-PC actually expire — and, just as usefully, which don't.** The calendar list already covered the domain, cards on file, and payment-API sunsets, but nothing said whether any of the machine's *logins* lapse on a timer. That question has a surprisingly tidy answer worth writing down once: only **three** things can stop working on a clock, and **two are one-time toggles** — disabling Tailscale key expiry (device keys expire every ~180 days by default, so a headless box silently drops off the network about six months in and you discover it when you urgently need remote access) and setting the Windows account password to never expire (it can inherit a 42-day maximum age depending on how the account was created; when it lapses RDP dies and you must be physically at the shop — `net accounts` tells you). The third is live and structural: **Google Drive for Desktop**. Its OAuth token persists indefinitely in normal use but is killed by changing the Google account password, revoking access, ~6 months of total inactivity, or a Workspace re-auth policy — and because Drive runs in a *user session* on a box that is headless and reboots itself around 4 AM, the "sign in again" prompt lands on a desktop nobody looks at and `G:\My Drive` stops being mounted. The doc calls out the non-obvious trap plainly (**changing your Google password stops the off-site backups**, an action that feels entirely unrelated to the POS) alongside the reassurance that it is *already alarmed* — [backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1) checks that `GoogleDriveFS` is running and soft-fails the healthcheck when the mirror doesn't happen, and local backups keep succeeding throughout, so only the off-site copy is affected. A second table records **what does not expire**, so this never has to be re-audited: the `SQUARE_ACCESS_TOKEN` is a Developer Dashboard token for the shop's *own* account and has **no timer** (the widely-cited 30-day expiry applies to OAuth tokens issued on behalf of *another* merchant — not this setup, per [square-terminal-setup.md](docs/square-terminal-setup.md)); Stripe keys are permanent until rotated; the Cloudflare Tunnel credentials `.json` holds a non-expiring secret and a *running* named tunnel never needs re-login (`cloudflared login` is only for creating or modifying tunnels); `JWT_SECRET` has no expiry; and Caddy's LAN certificates plus Cloudflare's edge certificates renew themselves with no action ever — Caddy's internal CA being offline, it doesn't even need internet to do so. The existing "Payment credentials" row was corrected accordingly: it had implied the Square token was OAuth and could expire, when it's really a *revocation and rotation* check. Also notes that staff PIN sessions (`POS_TTL`/`MANAGER_TTL`/`ADMIN_TTL` in [auth.js](src/lib/auth.js)) are meant to expire and aren't a fault. Docs only — no code touched.
- **Four longevity gaps closed: deploys now run the test suite, the monthly maintenance job watches drive health, the LAN-TLS root certificate is backed up, and dependency patching + account recovery are on the calendar.** The automated side of long-term operation was already strong (nightly verified backups, monthly VACUUM/prune, quarterly restore drill, healthchecks.io dead-man alerting, RUNBOOK §7A for calendar-expiry traps), so this pass targeted only what an audit of the actual scripts showed was genuinely missing. **(1) Test gate:** [scripts/deploy.ps1](scripts/deploy.ps1) went backup → build → restart, so the 9 test files covering money math, zone gating, credential lockout, order totals, loyalty and vouchers only ran when someone remembered to type `npm test` — precisely the regressions that don't crash but silently produce a wrong total or expose a staff route. A new step 3 runs `npm test` **after** the optional `-Pull` (so it tests the code actually shipping) and **before** the build (a failure costs ~0.3s instead of a full Next compile), gating on `$LASTEXITCODE` and never reaching `Restart-Service` — the live app keeps serving the old build throughout. A `-SkipTests` switch mirrors the existing `-SkipBackup` for genuine emergencies. **(2) Drive health:** [scripts/db-maintenance.ps1](scripts/db-maintenance.ps1) gains a fourth job reading `Get-PhysicalDisk` / `Get-StorageReliabilityCounter`. The backup script already alarms on a *full* disk ([backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1), <5 GB free); this catches a *dying* one on a box that writes 24/7 — doubly relevant on a used ex-corporate mini-PC. Two tiers so the alert stays worth reading: 80–89% wear or `HealthStatus=Warning` logs a `WARNING` into the normal monthly success ping ("plan a replacement"), while 90%+ wear, `Unhealthy`, or any uncorrected read/write errors raises `/fail` ("order a drive now"). Best-effort by design — many consumer/OEM SSDs don't expose wear through the Windows storage stack, so a missing counter logs `wear=n/a` **with the reason** and never fails the run or invents an alarm (verified: on an unelevated shell the counters are denied and the code degrades exactly as intended; the scheduled task runs `-RunLevel Highest`). **(3) LAN-TLS root:** `caddy-root-ca.crt` lived only on the mini-PC, in neither git nor the DB dump, yet it's the file needed to onboard a replacement tablet. [backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1) now copies it beside the dump and mirrors it to Drive under a fixed name (it rarely changes, so timestamped copies would just be clutter for the prune to chase). Deliberately **only the public certificate** — the CA *private key* stays on the machine and is documented as intentionally excluded, because this folder syncs to Google Drive and that key could mint certificates every till trusts; if it's ever lost, Caddy mints a new root and you re-trust 2–3 tablets in ~10 minutes, which is a better trade than a LAN-wide signing key in cloud storage. **(4) Calendar items:** RUNBOOK §7A gains a quarterly dependency-patch row with a runnable procedure (`npm audit` → `npm outdated` → patch/minor **within** the current major → `npm test` → deploy), plus the rules that majors are scheduled projects rather than quiet bumps and that a docket print must be tested after any Node major upgrade (`escpos`/`escpos-network` are pinned at unmaintained `3.0.0-alpha` releases with no upstream to fix them). This matters here specifically because [src/proxy.js](src/proxy.js) is Next.js **middleware** enforcing the public/staff zone split and the tunnel puts it on the internet — a Next security release can be patching the zone boundary itself. Two further rows cover **account recovery** (2FA recovery codes for registrar/Cloudflare/Stripe/Square/Google/healthchecks.io stored with the off-machine `.env` copy, plus a trusted second person — `.env` restores the app but does nothing for a lost phone) and the Caddy root. Docs updated: [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) (three backup files not two; the CA private key and cloudflared credentials added to "what the backup does NOT contain"; the fourth maintenance job), [lan-tls.md](docs/lan-tls.md) (a new *"If Caddy's data dir is lost"* section — Caddy silently mints a new root, every tablet errors the next morning, here's the ~10-minute recovery), and RUNBOOK §7. **(5) The quarterly restore drill could fail silently — now it can't.** Found while auditing the alerting: [restore-drill.ps1](scripts/restore-drill.ps1) was registered with `-WindowStyle Hidden` and wrote only to stdout, so its output went to a window that closes. On failure it threw, Task Scheduler recorded a non-zero `LastTaskResult`, and **nobody was told** — the error text was gone. That's backwards from every other job here, and it applied to the one whose entire purpose is proving the backups are recoverable: it could have been failing for a year, discovered only when a real restore was needed. It now uses the same `Send-Ping` + `Log` pattern as [backup-db.ps1](scripts/backup-db.ps1) and [db-maintenance.ps1](scripts/db-maintenance.ps1) — a pass pings the check, a failure pings `/fail` with the full run log, and every run appends to `logs\restore-drill.log` (gitignored; the file write is best-effort so a locked log can never cost the alert). Alerting reads `RESTORE_DRILL_HEALTHCHECK_URL` and falls back to the shared `BACKUP_HEALTHCHECK_URL`; the docs recommend a **dedicated** check with a ~90-day period, because sharing the daily backup check gets you failure emails but no dead-man's cover — nothing would notice the drill silently ceasing to run. Also logs the age of the dump being tested, and the failure message states plainly that nothing is broken *right now* but the backups are no longer proven restorable. **Verified by running it end-to-end on real data** with alerting redirected to a dead local address so the live healthcheck was untouched: pass path restored the 3-hour-old dump into the throwaway `restore_test`, counted 105 orders, cleaned up, exit 0; failure path (forced via an empty `BACKUP_DIR`) logged the diagnostic and exited 1; the dedicated-URL-wins precedence and the ping-failure warning path both confirmed. RUNBOOK §4G is now a three-way playbook covering a failed drill (with the "nothing is broken right now, but fix it before you need it" framing), and §7 plus [production-setup.md](docs/production-setup.md) note the new check. **(6) UPS: decided against, and recorded as a decision rather than deleted.** The docs recommended one in six places (DEPLOY §8, database-backup, go-live-checklist, migrate-to-new-pc, production-setup ×2). The owner's call: in a power cut the espresso machine, grinder, fridge, lights and Wi-Fi are all down, so the shop closes regardless — keeping the POS alive buys nothing. That's correct for the *availability* half of what a UPS was doing; the *other* half (clean shutdown protecting Postgres from mid-write corruption) is now written up as a knowingly accepted residual risk, bounded by Postgres being crash-safe by design and by the nightly verified backup capping worst-case loss at one trading day. Every UPS line is replaced by the **free stand-in that addresses the same failure**: leave Windows' *"Turn off write-cache buffer flushing on the device"* **unticked** on the system drive — a drive that lies about flushing is what actually turns a survivable power cut into a corrupt database. The reasoning lives once in [database-backup.md](docs/database-backup.md) under a heading that says not to re-raise it, with the other five docs pointing at it, so the recommendation doesn't quietly regrow the next time someone audits for resilience. Ops tooling and docs only — **no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched**; `npm test` 59/59 pass and all three edited `.ps1` files verified parse-clean.
- **[docs/production-setup.md](docs/production-setup.md) — a single ordered "zero to open for business" path that sequences every other doc, written to be navigable by an AI agent.** The repo had all the *pieces* of a production deployment documented well (rebrand, deploy, terminals, marketing, services, backups, monitoring, go-live) but no **map**: nothing said what order to do them in, which steps depend on which, or where one doc hands off to the next — so a first-time deploy meant reading a dozen documents to reconstruct the sequence, and the ordering constraints that actually bite were buried inside individual guides. The new doc is deliberately a **map, not a duplicate** — each phase states its goal, links the authoritative doc for the detail, and gives a "done when" so you know it's safe to move on, keeping the detail single-sourced so this doc can't rot out of sync. 13 phases: decide/gather → rebrand → host machine → app+DB → payments → printers → menu/staff/legal → public zone → marketing → LAN TLS → reboot resilience → backups/monitoring → rehearsal → opening day, then hands off to [RUNBOOK.md](RUNBOOK.md). Two things it adds that existed nowhere before: **(1)** an *"Ordering constraints (the ones that bite)"* table collecting the load-bearing sequencing in one place — Transform Rule **before** the tunnel (get it backwards and internet traffic is treated as LAN, exposing the staff zone), all tablets on HTTPS **before** `SESSION_COOKIE_SECURE=1` (a `Secure` cookie is dropped over plaintext → silent login failure), Postgres before the app service, the app before the tunnel/Caddy services, backups working before opening day, and never point the **apex** at Pages (it must stay on the tunnel or `/order` breaks); **(2)** an explicit **agent-vs-human split** — an agent may install prerequisites, clone/build, run the `scripts\*.ps1` installers and report verification, while a human owns the `.env` (never fabricate secrets), live card charges, DNS/tunnel cutover, legal wording (ABN, GST, ACCC surcharge, privacy `[REVIEW]` placeholders), and any commit — plus a hands-off list for the money/payment/zone files a deployment never needs to touch, and a resume-where-you-left-off state check for an agent picking the job up mid-way. Cross-linked from [README](README.md) and the top of [go-live-checklist.md](docs/go-live-checklist.md), which now says where to start if the shop PC isn't built yet. Docs only — no code touched.
- **Windows Update management for the 24/7 mini-PC POS host — automatic security patches, but no surprise reboots during trading and no unattended feature upgrades.** The box runs continuously behind a ~6am-6pm cafe, where Windows Update's defaults are the real risk: a mid-service forced reboot, or a 15-30 minute semi-annual "feature update" installing itself unannounced. New [scripts/configure-windows-updates.ps1](scripts/configure-windows-updates.ps1) sets machine-wide policy (Windows 11 Pro) to (1) hold active hours 06:00-18:00 so nothing auto-restarts during the trading day, (2) auto-install pending updates and force any restart at ~04:00 — after the 21:30 backup and 03:00 DB-maintenance jobs — via `AlwaysAutoRebootAtScheduledTime`, (3) auto-install monthly quality/security updates with a short deferral (default 4 days) so a bad Patch-Tuesday build is usually pulled before it lands, and (4) **pin the feature version** using `TargetReleaseVersion` (the reliable Win 11 Pro mechanism, not the flaky `DeferFeatureUpdatesPeriodInDays`) — the script auto-detects the box's current `DisplayVersion` and pins to it, so the big upgrade only happens when a human runs it on a closed day. Because a pinned version eventually falls out of servicing, [scripts/register-feature-update-reminder-task.ps1](scripts/register-feature-update-reminder-task.ps1) registers a durable **twice-yearly** Task Scheduler reminder (every 26 weeks — mirroring the quarterly restore-drill's `-WeeksInterval 13` pattern) that runs [scripts/feature-update-reminder.ps1](scripts/feature-update-reminder.ps1), logging to `logs/feature-update-reminder.log` and best-effort messaging any signed-in session; a Windows Scheduled Task, not an ephemeral in-session cron, so it survives reboots and the six-month horizon. All three scripts follow the existing `scripts/*.ps1` conventions (Administrator-guarded, `$ErrorActionPreference="Stop"`, idempotent re-register) and are written **strictly ASCII** to avoid the Windows-1252 BOM-less parse trap that silently broke `register-restore-drill-task.ps1` before. Verified parse-clean and ASCII-only; **not executed here** — these apply system-wide policy and power settings and are meant to be run on the mini-PC by an operator in an Administrator shell. Ops/deployment tooling only — no app, money, payment, auth, or zone logic touched.

### Security
- **LAN traffic between the Wi‑Fi tills and the mini‑PC is no longer plaintext — a Caddy reverse proxy terminates HTTPS on the LAN, and session cookies can now be marked `Secure`.** Closes the "still open" item the previous audit flagged (*"session cookies have no `secure` flag … the LAN runs over plain HTTP"*): the tills reached the POS over `http://192.168.1.10:3000`, so anyone who got onto the shop Wi‑Fi could sniff session cookies, PINs, and order data off the air. `next start` can't serve HTTPS itself, so [Caddy](../Caddyfile) now sits in front on `:443` and reverse-proxies to Next on `127.0.0.1:3000` — the tablet↔mini‑PC hop is encrypted and the mini‑PC↔Next hop stays on loopback (never on the wire). Certs are issued by **Caddy's internal CA** (`tls internal`) — fully offline, no internet needed to issue or renew, matching the shop's offline-resilience posture — with a one-time root-CA trust per iPad. The design was validated against the code before building, and it bolts on cleanly because **none of the app's security decisions are IP-based**: the public/LAN split keys on the `x-dreamy-zone` header ([zone.js](src/lib/zone.js)), LAN gating keys on the staff session cookie ([proxy.js](src/proxy.js)), and [requestUrl.js](src/lib/requestUrl.js) already honours `x-forwarded-proto`/`-host` — so a proxy in front changes nothing there.
